A Topos-Theoretic Semantics of Intuitionistic Modal Logic with an Application to the Logic of Branching Spacetime
Michael J. Lambert

Abstract
The Alexandrov topology affords a well-known semantics of modal necessity and possibility. This paper develops an Alexandrov topological semantics of intuitionistic propositional modal logic internally in any elementary topos. This is done by constructing interior and closure operators on the power-object associated to a given relation in the ambient topos. When the relation is an order, these operators model intuitionistic S4; when the relation is an equivalence relation, they also model the characteristic (B) axiom of classical S5. The running example of interest arises from the Branching space-time of Nuel Belnap, which is shown to induce a histories presheaf upon which can be defined an equivalence relation of being obviously undivided at a given point event. These results have some philosophical implications. For example, we study the branching space-time example in light of the indistinguishability interpretation of epistemic modal logic. We will also study several famous first-order formulas in presheaf topos semantics such as the so-called Barcan formula . We shall see, however, that one of the Barcan converses is invalidated by a simple example of non-trivial space-time branching. This invalidates a thesis of metaphysical actualism , namely, that there are no possibly existing but non-actual entities.



1
Introduction

Topos-theoretic developments of
modality
come in two flavors.
On the one hand, there are the
geometric modalities
manifesting as local
operators and leading to the theory of sites and sheaves. On the other hand,
there are various internalized incarnations of the usual modal operators
for necessity and possibility. These two have in common the goal of defining or
constructing certain morphisms in the topos structure that model the behavior
of the modality in question. Although local operators are well-studied, the
topos-theoretic accounts of necessity and possibility are fewer and less
standardized in their approaches. Perhaps this partly is owing to the nature of
modal logic itself. That is, the modal logic of possibility and necessity is
usually a classical, propositional logic, presented in a Hilbert-style
formalism, while the internal logic of toposes is in general intuitionistic,
higher-order, and presented in a sequent-calculus or type-theoretic formalism.
Consequently, there is not a great deal of consensus about what exactly
intuitionistic modal logic should be, much less a great deal of systematization
of, or even interest in, its categorical semantics.

In the interest of supplying this lack, this paper is a contribution to the
subject of the categorical semantics of intuitionistic modal logic. The goal is
to show how to internalize the ordinary semantics of the Alexandrov topology in
an arbitrary topos. This is done by starting with an internal relation and then
developing internalized interior and closure operators on the power-object
associated to the underlying object of the relation. When the relation is an
order, the power-object models intuitionistic S4; when the relation is also
symmetric, it models the (B) axiom which would be characteristic of
intuitionistic S5. These results are purely topos-theoretic analogues of the
standard Kripke semantics of modal logic
[
Kri63
]
phrased in terms of
the operators of topological semantics
[
MT44
]
associated to
orders. Although this is a sort of
back to basics
approach that may
appear outwardly naive or too simplistic, we believe that there are a variety
of useful constructions that come up along the way and that the overall
development illustrates well the use of Kripke-Joyal forcing semantics. There
are also applications and philosophical implications previewed below.

Investigation of the topos forcing semantics of modal operators is not exactly
new, but to our knowledge only three studies, namely,

[
AKK14
]
,
[
RZ91
]
and

[
Rey91
]
have done so in any depth. These have in common the
construction of certain
internal adjunctions
used to interpret the
necessity and possibility operators. In particular, all three studies interpret
necessity by a comonad associated to an internal frame homomorphism arising
from a given external geometric morphism either to or from the ambient topos in
question. Although such subsidiary geometric morphisms usually exist, or at
least can be cooked up, it seems worthwhile to develop a forcing semantics
without the assumption of any such geometric morphism. Such an account is
completely internal using only the given structure of the single fixed topos
viewed as a self-contained
universe of discourse
. The present attempt to
do so derives the required internal adjunctions from the topos structure
directly using only the assumption of the existence of an internal relation.
Such relations abound; for example, we may take the diagonal on a given object.
Our internalization of these topological semantics in the form of the
Alexandrov topology also has the satisfying outcome that it is intuitive and
formally very close to the ordinary set-theoretic version.

This paper is not one on the syntax or proof theory of modal logic. We do not
attempt to provide novel or state-of-the-art modal systems. For intuitionistic S4 we are happy simply to adopt the natural deduction system (IS4) of

[
BdP00
]
which has the satisfying feature of

substitutivity of proofs
. This system has a ready-made, sound categorical semantics, and our models will be shown to be of this type. When it comes to intuitionistic S5, there appears to be no corresponding system of natural deduction satisfying substitutivity of proofs. However, the distinguishing axiom of classical S5 is the (B) axiom which is modelled categorically by the MAO-couples of
[
Rey91
]
and
[
RZ91
]
. For this reason we skirt the question of giving a system (IS5) but think of MAO-couples as potential models of such a system, were it to be developed in subsequent future work. One main result is that any internal equivalence relation affords canonically an MAO-couple, hence a model of some hypothetical system (IS5).

Our application is to the logic of the so-called
branching space-time

[
Bel92
]
. This turns out to induce canonically an MAO-couple in a certain presheaf topos. This model stems from what we call the
histories presheaf
, defined on the underlying poset of branching space-time. The histories presheaf sends each point event of branching space-time to the set of all histories of that point event. The relation
obviously undivided
at a point event induces an equivalence relation on the histories presheaf, hence an MAO-couple. This model gives an especially satisfying gloss on modal statements with a distinctly epistemic flavor, which we envision as being relevant to formulating categorical epistemic modal logic generally. The way that this is achieved is by looking at the Kripke-Joyal forcing semantics of modal statements arising from the internal interior and closure operators associated to our Alexandrov topology constructions.

A simple example of non-trivial branching figures prominently in a concluding application to the analysis of several first-order formulas. In particular, we consider the so-called
Barcan formulas
and their converses
[
Bar47
]
which seem to be a perennial source of discussion in formal metaphysics and epistemology. Our analysis of these formulas is applied to existence predicates
[
Sco79
]
and briefly to the issue of
actualism
versus
possibilism
in the metaphysics of modality (stemming from the the introduction of possible world semantics to explain modality
[
Car47
]
,
[
Hin62
]
,
[
Kri63
]
,
[
Lew86
]
). Against the possibilist position that there exist non-actual entities, actualism asserts that there are no possibly existing but non-actual entities
[
Ada74
]
. We take the
actualist thesis
in a positive formulation to be

what possibly exists is actual

(A)

i.e. there are no merely possible but non-actual entities. Perhaps
unsurprisingly, our result is that non-trivial branching invalidates this
thesis. The reason is that non-trivial branching means that alternate histories
of a point event exist and are possible but not necessarily actual. The point
of our development is that the invalidation of the actualist thesis is
deducible from the introduced formalism of topos-theoretic modality and
non-trivial branching. The question as to whether this in any way settles the
debate turns upon whether branching space-time is a sound model and is not
taken up in this paper.

In more detail, some of our principal results and contributions are the following.

1.

Interior and closure operators associated to an internal relation
appear in
Definition

3.1
and

Definition

3.4
using certain naturally induced
morphisms on power-objects in the ambient topos. The interior operator is a
composition of a downset operator followed by an inverse image. Likewise,
the closure operator is a direct image followed by a join on the associated
power-object.

2.

The forcing semantics of these operators are the central ingredients
of our interpretation of modal necessity and possibility. The resulting
semantics appear in
Proposition

3.8
and

Corollary

3.11
. These forcing statements mimic
the ordinary set theoretic topological semantics of these operators in the
case of the Alexandrov topology but incorporate the characteristic forcing
element of
being true at a stage
.

3.

Along the way, in
Proposition

3.9
, we
clarify the forcing semantics of direct images in toposes. We also give a
forcing semantics of the join operator on a given internal power-object in

Proposition

3.10
. Together these
give our semantics of the Alexandrov closure, discussed immediately above.

4.

The main theoretical results of the paper are

Theorem

3.18
and

Theorem

3.21
which show that under
suitable conditions on the underlying relation, the associated power-object
with its interior and closure operators are categorical models of necessity
and possibility.

5.

The example of branching space-time
[
Bel92
]
, a synthetic
fusion of relativity and algebraic semantics of branching temporal logic,
is shown to induce canonically an equivalence relation in a certain
presheaf
topos, hence an MAO-couple, in
Theorem

3.26
.

6.

There appears to be some general philosophical import of our
constructions. This has its origins in unpacking the forcing semantics of
the modal operators in the context of presheaf categories and particularly
the example of branching space-time. The purpose of

Section

4.2
is to make an analogy the

indistinguishability interpretation
of necessity statement in modal
epistemic logic.

7.

The concluding discussion
Section

4.3

exhibits several first-order modal formulas for analysis. We shall see that
equivalence relations in toposes in particular always validate the
so-called
Barcan formula
, but by example that the converse does not
hold. Likewise, we shall see that
de re
existential statements
always imply
de dicto
existential statements but again by example
that the converse is not true. In the context of existence predicates, this
result thus says that whatever exists necessarily exists, but not
conversely. For this reason, branching space-time is not a model of

metaphysical actualism
.

The plan of the paper is as follows:
Section

2

gives an overview of our version of intuitionistic S4 and S5, recounts the
usual set-theoretic topological semantics of their classical versions, provides
an overview of basic topos theory and internal preorder theory in the form in
which we need it; the culmination is the definition of the topos-theoretic
algebraic models of our intuitionistic modal systems;

Section

3
presents our internalization of
the Alexandrov topology, including our construction of the interior and closure
operators and prove that they provide the appropriate algebraic models of
intuitionistic S4 and S5; finally
Section

4
shows that
the example of branching space-time induces a canonical equivalence relation on
the histories presheaf and thus as a result of the general theory affords a
model of intuitionistic S5.

2
Topos-Theoretic Algebraic Semantics

This section is mostly expository in nature. It summarizes the adopted system
(IS4) and after reviewing some topos theory gives a definition of the internalized
models of (IS4) and the models of the hypothetical system (IS5) in the form of
MAO-couples. We provide some subsidiary results and clarifications along the way
that will help in subsequent developments.

2.1
The System (IS4)

Consideration of the connections between space-time and modal logic probably
originate with
[
Gol80
]
. The modal system there is however classical and since we will be working in toposes ours ought to be
intuitionistic
. However, in dealing with modal logic, our purpose is neither to give a comprehensive presentation of the subject nor to exhibit a state-of-the-art system. It is rather merely to present a reasonable, minimal system to open the door to topos-interpretations of possibility and necessity with a minimal assumption of available structure. In particular, we do not assume the existence of a base topos, or any extra external geometric morphisms as in
[
Rey91
]
and
[
AKK14
]
, although these are usually available.

The system of choice is
intuitionistic S4
(IS4) of

[
BdP00
]
. Specifically, we assume throughout an intuitionistic propositional logic
\bot
,
\top
,
\neg\phi
,
\phi\wedge\psi
,
\phi\vee\psi
,
\phi\supset\psi
, augmented by modal necessity
\Box\phi
and possibility
\Diamond\phi
operators subject to the axioms of the usual intuitionistic calculus (IPC) plus the further modal axioms, namely,

1.

\Box(\phi\supset\psi)\supset(\Box\phi\supset\Box\psi)

2.

\Box\phi\supset\phi
and
\phi\supset\Diamond\phi

3.

\Box\phi\supset\Box\Box\phi

4.

\Box(\phi\supset\Diamond\psi)\supset(\Diamond\phi\supset\Diamond\psi)
.

The natural deduction calculus for (IS4) is detailed in

[
BdP00
, §4]
where it is shown to satisfy

substitutivity of proofs
, a feature missing from previous accounts of
intuitionistic S4 (e.g.
[
Pra06
, Chapter VI]
).
Our choice of this system is due to the fact that
[
BdP00
]

provides a sound categorical semantics of (IS4) in the form of certain
distinguished
categorical models
. This is reviewed and internalized in

Definition

2.11
. A main result of this paper
(
Theorem

3.18
) is that any order relation in a topos affords
a categorical model of (IS4) in this sense.

Our examples prompt consideration of certain additional modal axioms. The
classical system
Diodorean S4
of
[
Gol80
]
is also the
modal system of knowledge and belief of
[
Sta06
]
. This is built on
classical propositional logic and includes the comonad axioms for
\Box

(the monad axioms for
\Diamond
follow classically from inter-definability),
along with the
distributive law
, namely,
\Diamond\Box\phi\supset\Box\Diamond\phi
. This modal axiom is sometimes labeled (C) since the
corresponding frame condition is a convergence law on the associated relation,
saying that two elements with a common lower bound also have a common upper
bound:

x\leq y\text{ and }x\leq z\text{ implies }y\leq w\text{ and }z\leq w\text{ for some }w.

The modal system (IS4) augmented by the distributive law (C) will be called

distributive (IS4)
. The categorical semantics of (IS4) and
distributive (IS4) is discussed in
Section

2.3
and
specifically
Definition

2.11
where we adopt the categorical
models of
[
BdP00
]
.

The example of
branching space-time

[
Bel92
]
models a more
specific modal system that we are tempted to call
intuitionistic S5

(IS5). This would hypothetically be a variation on (IS4) above plus the
further axiom scheme (B)

\phi\supset\Box\Diamond\phi\text{ and }\Diamond\Box\phi\supset\phi.

(2.1)

which is the characteristic scheme added to classical S4 to obtain classical S5.
However, although there are several proposal for a system (IS5) (e.g.
[
Pra06
]
,
[
Sim94
]
,
[
AMdPR01
]
) no such system with a natural deduction calculus satisfying substitutivity of proofs appears to exist. For this reason we bracket the question of presenting a system (IS5) but think of some hypothetical system as minimally (IS4) plus (B). Our preference is to switch to an existing categorical semantics and take the MAO-couples (
Definition

2.13
) of
[
Rey91
]
and
[
RZ91
]
as likely candidates for a class of models of such a hypothetical system. This is because MAO-couples are preorder categorical models of (IS4)
(
Lemma

2.15
) for which the relevant structure forms
an internal adjoint modality modeling the distinguishing (B) axiom. One of our
main results (
Theorem

3.21
) is that any
equivalence relation in a topos
\mathscr{E}
induces in a canonical way an MAO-couple,
hence what we expect will be a categorical model of a system (IS5).

Our models are based on the traditional semantics of classical S4 originating with
[
Kri63
]
,
[
Kri65
]
. Classical S4 is interpreted via an
accessibility relation

R
on a set
X
that is at least reflexive and transitive. We shall refer to such a relation as an
order
. Accessibility relations interpreting classical S5 are equivalence relations, that is, those relations that are reflexive, transitive and also symmetric. The related
topological semantics
originate with
[
MT44
]
and are summarized in
[
AK08
, §1.3]
,
[
Kis11
, §2.2]
and
[
Kis17
, §3.2]
where it is shown in particular how topological semantics recover and generalize those of Kripke. In this set up, let
(X,\mathscr{O}(X))
denote a topological space with
\mathscr{O}(X)
the frame of open sets. Each proposition is interpreted as a subset
\llbracket\phi\rrbracket\subset X
in such a way that

1.

\llbracket\neg\phi\rrbracket=X\setminus\llbracket\phi\rrbracket

2.

\llbracket\phi\wedge\psi\rrbracket=\llbracket\phi\rrbracket\cap\llbracket\psi\rrbracket

3.

\llbracket\phi\vee\psi\rrbracket=\llbracket\phi\rrbracket\cup\llbracket\psi\rrbracket

4.

\llbracket\phi\supset\psi\rrbracket=\llbracket\phi\rrbracket\multimap\llbracket\psi\rrbracket

where ‘
\multimap
’ here denotes the implication operator on the Boolean algebra powerset
PX
. The box operator
\Box\phi
is interpreted using the associated interior operator

\mathsf{int}\colon PX\to\mathscr{O}(X)\qquad\qquad S\mapsto\bigcup\{U\in\mathscr{O}(X)\mid U\subset S\}

taking a subset
S\subset X
to the union of all open sets contained in
S
.
That is, declare

\llbracket\Box\phi\rrbracket:=\mathsf{int}\llbracket\phi\rrbracket.

Likewise,
\Diamond
is interpreted using the closure operator
\mathsf{cl}\colon PX\to\mathscr{O}(X)
, namely, the intersection of all closed sets containing a given set. The usual semantics is that the sequent
\phi\vdash\psi
is valid if, and only if,
\llbracket\phi\rrbracket\subset\llbracket\psi\rrbracket
holds in
PX
.

A particular example is the so-called
Alexandrov topology
associated to
any set with a relation
[
Joh82
, §II,1.8]
. Let
(X,\leq)
denote
an order and declare the opens to be the upsets: those
U\subset X
such that

x\in U
and
x\leq y
implies
y\in U
too. Consequently, the closed sets are
the
downsets
. We then have the following element-wise descriptions of
closure and interior:

s\in\mathsf{int}(S)\text{ if, and only if, }s\leq x\text{ implies }x\in S

(2.2)

and

s\in\mathsf{cl}(S)\text{ if, and only if, }s\leq x\text{ for some }x\in S.

(2.3)

Thus, interpreting
\Box
as
\mathsf{int}
, we have the Alexandrov semantics of
necessity, namely,

\displaystyle s\in\llbracket\Box p\rrbracket

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }s\in\mathsf{int}\llbracket p\rrbracket

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }x\in\llbracket p\rrbracket\text{ for all $x$ such that }s\leq x.

Likewise, interpreting the possibility operator
\Diamond
as Alexandrov closure,
we have the existential semantics

\displaystyle s\in\llbracket\Diamond p\rrbracket

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }s\in\mathsf{cl}\llbracket p\rrbracket

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }x\in\llbracket p\rrbracket\text{ for some $x$ such that }s\leq x

Thinking of a proposition
S\subset X
being true for an individual
x\in X
if, and only if,
x\in S
, this reproduces the original semantics of
\Box
given in
[
Kri63
]
. The goal of this paper is to construct an internal Alexandrov topology for any object with a relation in an arbitrary topos. To this end, we need to construct associated interior and closure operators and to better understand their forcing semantics. This will be done in

Section

3.1
and
Section

3.2

below.

2.2
Toposes and Their Internal Logic

Since our goal is to internalize the Alexandrov topology, we must set out the
main features of elementary toposes that will figure in our development. Our
reference is
[
MM92
]
. Briefly, a topos is a finitely
complete category
\mathscr{E}
with a subobject classifier
\Omega
and
power-objects.

Definition 2.1
.

The
power object
of an object
X\in\mathscr{E}
is an object
PX

together with a membership morphism
\in_{X}\colon X\times PX\to\Omega
with
the property that for any
f\colon X\times Y\to\Omega
there is a unique
morphism
\hat{f}
making

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times PX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\hat{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commute. Call
\hat{f}
the
transpose
of
f
. ∎

By uniqueness, for any such morphism
f
, we have
f=1_{X}\in_{X}\hat{f}
. As a
special case, we recover
P1\cong\Omega
.

Example 2.2
.

The categories
\mathbf{Set}
,
\mathbf{FinSet}
, and
\mathbf{Set}^{\to}
are all
toposes. ∎

Example 2.3
.

Recall that a presheaf on
\mathscr{C}
is a functor
\mathscr{C}^{op}\to\mathbf{Set}
.
Together with natural transformations as morphisms these comprise a topos
of presheaves
[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}]
. Likewise, any category of sheaves
(presheaves with a unique amalgamation property)
\mathsf{Sh}(\mathscr{C},J)

on a site
(\mathscr{C},J)
is a topos. Presheaf toposes are treated in detail
in
Section

4.1
. ∎

For any topos
\mathscr{E}
, the subobject classifier
\Omega
is an internal Heyting
algebra. The binary meet operation
\wedge
in particular is the classifying
morphism of
\langle\top,\top\rangle\colon 1\to\Omega\times\Omega
where

\top\colon 1\to\Omega
is the top element. Write
\top_{W}\colon W\to\Omega

for the arrow
\top\circ!\colon W\to 1\to\Omega
, thought of, as
true
relative to
W
. The internal preorder on
\Omega
is the equalizer of

\wedge
and the first projection. The Heyting implication
\Rightarrow\colon\Omega\times\Omega\to\Omega
is the classifying morphism of the order
subobject. The internal equality on
\Omega
is the classifying morphism of the
diagonal
\Delta\colon\Omega\to\Omega\times\Omega
.

By
[
MM92
, Theorem IV.8.1]
, each power object
PX
is also
an internal Heyting algebra and the assignment

g\colon W\to PX\qquad\mapsto\qquad\in_{X}(1\times g)

is the unique one inducing a natural isomorphism of external Heyting algebras

\mathscr{E}(W,PX)\cong\mathscr{E}(W\times X,\Omega)

(2.4)

whose reverse correspondence is transposition. In particular, each
PX
has an
internal preorder given again as the equalizer of the meet and first projection.
More on internal preorders following
Definition

2.5
below.

The power-object operation extends to a functor
P\colon\mathscr{E}^{op}\to\mathscr{E}
whose arrow assignment is given by sending a given arrow
f
to the
transpose
Pf
appearing in the diagram

Uniqueness makes this a contravariant functor on
\mathscr{E}
. Call
Pf
the

inverse image
morphism associated to
f
. In ordinary set theory, this
morphism has the action

Pf(T)=\{a\in A\mid f(a)\in T\}

(2.5)

which is the usual inverse image morphism.

Associated to each such arrow there is also a
direct image

\exists_{f}\colon PX\to PY
. First let
m\colon U_{X}\to X\times PX
denote the
subobject classified by
\in_{X}
and then
\exists_{f}
is the transpose in the
diagram on the right:

That is, first classify the image of
(f\times 1)m
via
\phi
and then define

\exists_{f}
to be the transpose of that classifying map. In ordinary set
theory, this has the effect

\exists_{f}S=\{b\in B\mid b=f(a)\text{ for some }a\in A\}.

(2.6)

Calculations will depend upon the following result, whose proof is
straightforward by the constructions of the inverse and direct image morphisms
above.

Lemma 2.4
.

For any morphism
f\colon A\to B
,

1.

(a\in_{A}P(f)(s))=(f(a)\in_{B}s)

2.

(a\in_{A}s)=(f(a)\in_{PX}\exists_{f}(s))

for any element
\langle a,s\rangle\colon W\to A\times PA
.∎

We will have need of some generalities on preordered objects in toposes (cf.

[
Joh14
]
,
[
MM92
]
, and
[
Woo04
]
). By a
relation
r\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}Y
in a topos we mean a monomorphism
r\colon R\to X\times Y
, or, equivalently, a pair of jointly monic arrows
R\to X
,
R\to Y
. Such relations are ordered by
s\leq r
if, and only if,
s\colon S\to X\times Y
factors through
r\colon R\to X\times Y
by a (necessarily) monic
arrow
S\to R
. Relations
s\colon X{\mathrel{\to}}Y
and
r\colon Y{\mathrel{\to}}Z
compose by
first taking a pullback as at left below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\times_{Y}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\times_{Y}R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s\otimes r}}

and then taking an image of the product map formed by the leftmost and
rightmost projections as on the right above.

Definition 2.5
.

An
internal preorder
is a pair
(A,r)
consisting of an object
A

and a relation
r\colon A{\mathrel{\to}}A
that is internally reflexive and
transitive in the sense that

1.

\Delta_{A}\leq r

2.

r\otimes r\leq r

both hold. These are the
reflexivity
and
transitivity

axioms respectively. An
internal equivalence relation
is a
reflexive and transitive relation
r
on
A
that is also symmetric in the
sense that
r^{\dagger}\leq r
where
r^{\dagger}
is the reversed relation
given by composing
r
with the canonical isomorphism
X\times X\cong X\times X
interchanging the factors of the product. ∎

Any internal preorder is an internal category. An internal functor is an
order-preseving morphism. We will write ‘
\leq_{r}
’ for the classifying arrow of

r
and ‘
f\leq_{r}g
’ to mean that the generalized elements
f,g\colon X\to A

factor through the order
r
as in

and

internal functors. These arise as certain transposes:

The
upsegment
arrow
\uparrow\colon L\to PL
is the transpose of
the classifying map of the opposite order
r^{\dagger}
. ∎

Definition 2.7
.

An
adjunction
between preordered objects
(P,r)
and
(Q,s)
in a
topos
\mathscr{E}
is a pair of morphisms
f\colon P\rightleftarrows Q:g

satisfying the two usual identities
1\leq_{r}gf
and
fg\leq_{s}1
. ∎

Both segment arrows are internal functors.

Example 2.8
.

For any morphism
f\colon A\to B
, the direct and inverse s are
internally adjoint with
\exists_{f}\dashv Pf
. Any such inverse image also
has a right adjoint
\forall_{f}\colon PA\to PB
. At least when the morphism
is the unique one
A\to 1
, this has a nice description as the classifying
map of the top element of
PA
. In this case, denote the quantifiers as

\forall_{A},\exists_{A}\colon PA\rightrightarrows\Omega
. For a morphism

\phi\colon X\to\Omega
thought of as a proposition with a single argument

\phi(x)
varying over entities of type
X
, define
\forall x\phi(x)
and

\exists x\phi(x)
to be the morphisms

\forall_{X}\hat{\phi},\exists_{X}\hat{\phi}\colon 1\rightrightarrows\Omega

where
\hat{\phi}
is the transpose
1\to PX
of
\phi
. ∎

Certain structures in toposes are defined by such internal adjunctions. For
example, we will see that the power-objects
PX
in any topos are internal
sup-lattices in the following sense.

Definition 2.9

(§5.3
[
Joh14
]
)
.

An
internal sup-lattice
is an internal poset
(A,r)
together with an internal left adjoint
\bigvee\colon PA\to A
for
\downarrow\colon A\to PA
. Refer to
\bigvee
as the
join operator
. Such
(A,r)
is
internally cocomplete
. A
homomorphism
of sup lattices
f\colon(A,r)\to(B,s)
is a morphism
f\colon A\to B
that preserves sups in the sense that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee}

commutes. Let
\mathbf{sLat}(\mathscr{E})
denote the category of sup lattices internal to
\mathscr{E}
. ∎

Appropriate compositions of left and right adjoints have the structure of
internal monads or comonads. Our internal interior and closure operators will
be examples. The comonad and monad equations are precisely those which validate
the axioms for necessity and possibility.

Definition 2.10
.

A
comonad
on an preordered object
(X,r)
is an order-preserving
morphism
d\colon X\to X
satisfying both
d\leq_{r}1_{X}
and
d\leq_{r}dd
.
The first is the
counit law
and the second is the

comultiplication law
. Dually, a
monad

t\colon X\to X
is
an order-preserving morphism satisfying the dual
unit law

1_{X}\leq_{r}c
and
multiplication law

cc\leq_{r}c
.∎

2.3
Topos Models of (IS4) and (IS5)

As previously discussed, our intention is not to present a novel version of
intuitionistic modal logic, but merely to adopt a system with an established
categorical semantics justifying its robustness. To this end, we internalize
the categorical semantics of (IS4) from

[
BdP00
, Definition 5]
. There a
categorical model
is
defined as a cartesian closed category with coproducts together with a monoidal
comonad and a monad satisfying a strength axiom. Here
monoidal comonad

means a lax cartesian monoidal comonad whose structure transformations are also
cartesian monoidal. This definition involves a number of coherence axioms, all
of which can be internalized. However, in the interest of readability, we will
suppress explicit presentation of these since they are automatically validated
by our internal preorder models. We do take the notion of internal category,
functor and transformation for granted
[
Joh14
, Ch.2]
.

Throughout fix a topos
\mathscr{E}
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
d\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
denote an internal monoidal comonad on
an internal category
\mathbb{C}
. A
d
-strong
monad on

\mathbb{C}
is a monad
t
on
\mathbb{C}
together with an internal natural
transformation

s\colon d\times t\Rightarrow t(d\times 1)

satisfying the four axioms of
[
BdP00
, Definition 4.2]
.
A
categorical model of (IS4)
is an internal cartesian closed
category
\mathbb{C}
with coproducts together with a monoidal comonad

d\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
and a
d
-strong monad
t\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
. A
categorical order model of (IS4)
is one
where
\mathbb{C}
is an internal preorder. ∎

Remark 2.12
.

In any preorder with appropriate endofunctors, strength takes the form of
the inequality

d\wedge t\leq t(d\wedge 1).

and the coherence axioms of the reference are vacuous. ∎

A main result of
[
BdP00
]
is that when
\mathscr{E}=\mathbf{Set}
such a
structure affords a sound categorical interpretation of (IS4)

[
BdP00
, Proposition 7]
. For this reason, we think of the
class of structures in
Definition

2.11
as providing our
categorical semantics of (IS4).

As our categorical semantics of (IS5), we take a version of the MAO-couples of

[
Rey91
]
and
[
RZ91
]
. Again we emphasize that we are
not adopting any particular presentation of (IS5) since there appears to be
little if any consensus about what such a system would look like. A defining
characteristic however should be the (B)-axiom which is modeled by the
requirement that the monad and comonad in a categorical model of (IS4) form an

adjoint modality
. Since MAO-couples have such an adjoint modality as a
defining feature, we take them as our semantics. We phrase the definition as in

[
Rey91
, §2.4]
explicitly in terms of internal preorders.

Definition 2.13
.

An
MAO-couple
is an internal Heyting algebra
A
together with
an idempotent comonad
d\colon A\to A
and an idempotent monad

t\colon A\to A
satisfying the two axioms

1.

t\dashv d

2.

t(1\wedge d)=t\wedge d

where
\wedge
is the internal meet on
A
. ∎

Remark 2.14
.

The statement that
t
is left adjoint to
d
means that two inequalities
hold, namely, (B.1)
1\leq dt
, what we will call the
unit law
,
and (B.2)
td\leq 1
, the
counit law
. Our order examples always
satisfy (B.2) but (B.1) only under the further condition
that the order
(X,\leq)
is an equivalence relation. ∎

Lemma 2.15
.

Any MAO-couple is a categorical model of (IS4).

Proof.

Any internal Heyting algebra is internally cartesian closed and cocomplete.
The adjoint modality forces
d
to be internally (cartesian) monoidal.
Finally, the second axiom
t(1\wedge d)=t\wedge d
implies that
t
is

d
-strong.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

A categorical order model of (IS4) is
distributive
if additionally the
distributive law

td\leq dt
holds. Distributivity is meant to encode a further relationship between the monad and comonad beyond the deducible
d\leq t
. (IS4) is not assumed to be distributive. The distributive law above is meant to interpret the further modal axiom (C) presented as

\Diamond\Box p\supset\Box\Diamond p

mentioned for example in discussion of the Diodorean system (S4.2) from

[
Gol80
]
. Any symmetric order satisfies (C). Distributive laws
between a monad and a comonad have been studied 2-categorically

[
PW99
]
,
[
PW02
]
, and
[
Kos05
]
.
Generally, a complete statement of such distributivity requires further
coherence conditions. These are omitted here since they are satisfied
trivially by our preordered internal categories. Note that MAO-couples are
automatically distributive. ∎

2.4
Branching Space-time

One of our main results is that given an equivalence relation
(X,\approx)
in
a topos, the power object
PX
is an MAO-couple
(
Theorem

3.21
). We shall close this section by
introducing the setting that will yield one specific such model, namely,
the
branching space-time
of
[
Bel92
]
. The relation

obviously undivided
(
Definition

3.22
) will the relevant
equivalence relation.

Let
\mathscr{W}
denote an ordinary poset, viewed as a category, called

our world
. Denote the order as ‘
\leq
’. Think of elements

e\in\mathscr{W}
as
point events
with
e\leq e^{\prime}
meaning that from the
perspective of
e^{\prime}
, event
e
did occur; and that from the perspective of
e
,
the event
e^{\prime}
may or might occur. We think of such
e
and
e^{\prime}
as being

causally related
. Causality is formalized in the requirement that
\leq

is a
partial order
. In Minkowski space-time, causal precedence is a
partial order given by certain future-directed null or future-directed timelike
vectors. This is to be contrasted with the models of
[
Gol80
]
which
are just orders and thus model
chronological precedence
and are aptly
named
time-frames
.

Recall
[
Mac98
, §IX.1]
that a directed subset of a poset
\mathscr{P}

is a set of elements
D\subset\mathscr{P}_{0}
such that any
x,y\in D
have a common
element
z\in D
with
x\leq z
and
y\leq z
– briefly, if every pair of
elements of
D
have a common upper bound contained in
D
.

Definition 2.17
.

A
history
is a proper maximal directed subset
H\subset\mathscr{W}_{0}
. Let
H_{e}
denote the set of histories of a point event
e\in\mathscr{W}
.
∎

By Zorn’s Lemma, every directed set can be extended to a history. In
particular, every point event belongs to some history. So, each
H_{e}
is never
empty. Histories are
downward-closed
: given
e^{\prime}\in H
and
e\leq e^{\prime}
, we
must have
e\in H
too. For
\{e\}\cup H
is directed and so must
be
H
by maximality. So, as a result, each history is a
maximal ideal

in our world; conversely, each such maximal ideal is a history. For this
reason, we can write
\mathbf{mSpec}(\mathscr{W})
for the set of histories of our world.
Although it is not strictly necessary for our theoretical development, we may
take each history to be a
Minkowski spacetime
– that is, a
4-dimensional real manifold with a peculiar metric attached to it. Histories in
branching space-time organize into a presheaf on our world (cf.

Example

2.3
). We will call this
presheaf the
histories presheaf
. The proof is essentially a matter of
unpacking some previous observations.

Lemma 2.18
.

The assignment
H_{(-)}\colon\mathscr{W}^{op}\to\mathbf{Set}
given by
e\mapsto H_{e}
is a presheaf.

Proof.

That histories are downward-closed amounts to the fact that for causally related events
e_{1}\leq e_{2}
, it follows that
H_{e_{2}}\subset H_{e_{1}}
holds. By reflexivity and transitivity of the causal order, this assignment defines a presheaf on our world.
∎

A running example that will figure in later considerations is the following. It
is admittedly contrived, but is meant to illustrate the simplest example of
non-trivial branching.

Example 2.19
.

Let
\mathscr{W}
denote the category with four objects and three non-identity
arrows as suggested by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{:=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

In particular
\mathscr{W}
is a poset. It is not intended to be a
real

branching space-time, but just a toy model that will be used to illustrate
our later examples. Presumably any space-time with nontrivial branching
would admit at least one order-preserving morphism from
\mathscr{W}
. Let

h_{1}
and
h_{2}
denote the histories

h_{1}=\{e_{-1}\leq e_{0}\leq e_{1}\}\qquad h_{2}=\{e_{-1}\leq e_{0}\leq e_{1}\}

thought of respectively as
h_{1}
occurs
and
h_{2}
occurs
.
Note that these are the only two histories. The histories presheaf
therefore makes the following assignments:

\displaystyle H_{e_{-1}}

\displaystyle=H_{e_{0}}=\{h_{1},h_{2}\}

\displaystyle H_{e_{1}}

\displaystyle=\{h_{1}\}

\displaystyle H_{e_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{h_{2}\}

A complete picture of the image of
H
in
\mathbf{Set}
is therefore

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{h_{1}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{h_{1}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{h_{1},h_{2}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{h_{1},h_{2}\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cdot\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cdot\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\cdot\gamma}

where the hooked arrows are the singleton inclusions and
-\cdot\gamma
is
in fact just the identity arrow. We shall return to this example
particularly in
Section

4.3
where it will be
used to study first-order modal formulas. ∎

3
Internalized Alexandrov Topology

This section presents our foundational theoretical results. As the title
suggests, these consist in internalizing the Alexandrov topology in an
arbitrary topos. The point is that certain interior and closure operators
on power objects can be associated to any suitably ordered object in a topos.
Once these are constructed, we prove internalized identities that will
eventually validate the laws of intuitionistic S4 and S5 under suitable
interpretation.

3.1
Interior and Closure Internally

Internal orders and their up- and downsegment arrows
(
Definition

2.6
) lead to what will be our
semantics of the
\Box
-operator. Note that this definition does not require
the relation in
(X,r)
to be reflexive or transitive.

Definition 3.1
.

The
interior operator
associated to a relation
(X,r)
is
the composite morphism

\mathsf{int}:=P(\uparrow)\circ\downarrow\colon PX\to PX.

where
P(\uparrow)\colon P^{2}X\to PX
is the result of the power-object
functor
P\colon\mathscr{E}^{op}\to\mathscr{E}
applied to the upsegment arrow

\uparrow\colon X\to PX
and
\downarrow\colon P^{2}X\to PX
is the
downsegment arrow for the internal Heyting algebra
PX
as in

Definition

2.6
. ∎

Remark 3.2
.

Note that in ordinary set theory, when the relation is a preorder, using
the inverse image equation

Equation

2.5
, this composite has the
effect

P(\uparrow)\!\downarrow\!\!(S)=\{x\in X\mid\uparrow\!\!x\subset S\}=\{x\in X\mid x\leq y\text{ implies }y\in S\}

(3.1)

for any
S\subset X
, which is precisely the interior in the Alexandrov
topology on
(X,r)
as calculated in

Equation

2.2
. One purpose
of this section is to justify the notation by showing that
\mathsf{int}
is
an internal comonad in any topos
\mathscr{E}
. This will be done explicitly in

Section

3.3
. ∎

Now, we shall develop the closure operator on
PX
. This depends on the fact
that each power-object
PX
is an internal sup lattice as in

Definition

2.9
. One construction of the join is given in

[
Joh02
, §B2.3]
using the machinery of fibered limits and colimits. This is theoretically elegant, but would require a lengthy 2-categorical digression to unpack the construction in a useful way. Another approach is that of
[
Woo04
, §5.4]
but does not quite work with our present set-up. The most conducive and relevant explicit construction is given in
[
Joh14
, §5.3]
and is due originally to
[
Mik76
]
.

Construction 3.1
.

The first point to consider is that
\Omega
itself is an internal sup lattice. That it is an internal Heyting algebra is proved for example in

[
MM92
]
. The internal join is then defined as

\textstyle\bigvee_{\Omega}:=\Omega^{\top}\colon P\Omega\to\Omega

(3.2)

and shown to make
\Omega
internally cocomplete in the proof of

[
Joh14
, Lemma 5.35]
. More precisely, the join operator on

\Omega
is constructed as a transpose as in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\times\Omega^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\times P\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\times P\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\Omega^{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{\Omega}}

as in the definition of the contravariant exponentiation functor

A^{-}\colon\mathscr{E}^{op}\to\mathscr{E}
for any object
A
. The work of the
general construction of the join on
PX
is arriving at a subsidiary
classifying morphism
\phi
whose transpose composed with the join on

\Omega
transposes to the desired join on
PX
. For this, consider the
subobject classified by the membership arrow on
PX
:

\hbox to121.69pt{\vbox to53.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.84366pt\lower-27.52058pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Set-theoretically,
U_{PX}
is the collection of pairs consisting of a
subset of
X
and a collection of subsets of
X
to which the former
belongs;
c
projects that set while
d
projects the collection. Now, form
the product arrow of the transposes of
c
and of
1_{X}\times d
, and let

\phi
denote the classifying arrow of its image:

Transpose
\phi
to
\hat{\phi}\colon X\times P^{2}X\to P\Omega
and then the
join on
PX
is the unique arrow
\bigvee_{PX}
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times PX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times P^{2}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\bigvee_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\textstyle\bigvee_{PX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{X}}

commute. The point of
[
Joh14
, Proposition 5.36]
is showing that this indeed provides an appropriate internal left adjoint. ∎

Remark 3.3
.

In the next subsection we will show that this join operator has a
reasonably intuitive semantics. We shall do this in the next subsection.
For now, we discuss a subtlety. Set theoretically, an element or entity
x

is a member of a union of sets in a collection
\mathcal{S}
if, and only
if, there is a particular set in that collection
\mathcal{S}
of which
x

is a member:

x\in\bigcup_{X\in\mathcal{S}}X\text{ if, and only if, there is }X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\text{ such that }x\in X^{\prime}.

From the internal construction above, however, it is not at all immediately
clear that the existential part of the above statement is a feature of the
semantics of the elementary join on
PX
. As a first pass, putting together
the construction of the join on
PX
and the join on
\Omega
, we have a
diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times PX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times P^{2}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\times P\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\bigvee}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\top_{P\Omega},1\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee}

implying that for any element
\langle x,s\rangle\colon W\to X\times P^{2}X
,
we have an equivalence

\top_{W}=x\in_{PX}\bigvee s\text{ if, and only if, }\top_{W}=\top_{W}\in_{\Omega}\hat{\phi}(x,s).

(3.3)

It turns out with a little further work that the existential element can in
fact be extracted from the construction. This will be done in

Proposition

3.10

once we have introduced Kripke-Joyal semantics. ∎

Definition 3.4
.

For any relation
(X,r)
, the
closure operator
associated to
r
is
the composite

\displaystyle\mathsf{cl}:=\textstyle\bigvee\exists_{\downarrow}\colon PX\to PX

(3.4)

where
\bigvee
is the join on
PX
and
\exists_{\downarrow}
is the direct
image of
\downarrow\colon X\to PX
. ∎

Remark 3.5
.

Set-theoretically, using the computation in

Equation

2.6
, this operator has the
effect on a subset
S\subset X

\displaystyle\textstyle\bigvee_{PX}\exists_{\downarrow}S

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{T\in PX\mid T=\downarrow\!\!s\text{ for some }s\in S\}

(3.5)

\displaystyle=\bigcup\{\downarrow\!\!s\mid s\in S\}

(3.6)

\displaystyle=\{x\in X\mid x\leq s\text{ for some }s\in S\}

(3.7)

which is precisely the Alexandrov closure of
S
as calculated in
Equation

2.3
. ∎

3.2
Operator Semantics

Here we extend the well-known Kripke-Joyal forcing semantics to the interior
and closure operators of the previous section. This does require some fairly
detailed calculations, but the upshot is that we obtain familiar-looking
semantics of interior and closure, as well as satisfying proofs that the
constructions validate the monad and comonad axioms given appropriate
conditions on the underlying relation. Along the way, we will give some
subsidiary results facilitating our calculations. Although the results for the
closure and interior operators appear to be new, we make no claim to
originality with regard to the required lemmas. Their importance in the
development however warrants complete proofs.

Define the
forcing relation
‘
W\Vdash\phi(a)
’, read
W
forces

\phi
at
a
, to hold just in case the generalized element
a\colon W\to X

factors through the subobject classified by the proposition
\phi\colon X\to\Omega
as in

In other words,
W
forces
\phi
at
a
is understood as the relation

W\Vdash\phi(a)\text{ if, and only if, }\phi(a)=\top_{W}.

A central result of topos theory is that this relation extends to the logic of

\Omega
. We will only need the special cases of conjunction, implication and
quantification, so these are quoted from standard references in the following
result.

Theorem 3.6

(Forcing Semantics)
.

Let
a\colon W\to X
denote any element and
\phi,\psi\colon X\rightrightarrows\Omega
propositions on
X
. Then

1.

W\Vdash\phi(a)\wedge\psi(a)
if, and only if,
W\Vdash\phi(a)
and
W\Vdash\psi(a)

2.

W\Vdash\phi(a)\Rightarrow\psi(a)
if, and only if,
V\Vdash\psi(ab)
whenever
V\Vdash\phi(ab)
for any arrow
b\colon V\to W
.

If
\phi\colon X\times Y\to\Omega
is a proposition in two unknowns, then

1.

W\Vdash\forall y\phi(a,y)
if, and only if,
V\Vdash\phi(ap,b)
for all generalized elements
p\colon V\to W
and
b\colon V\to Y

2.

W\Vdash\exists y\phi(a,y)
if, and only if,
V\Vdash\phi(ap,b)
for some epimorphism
p\colon V\twoheadrightarrow W
and some element
b\colon V\to Y
.

Proof.

This is proved in standard texts. For example, see the proof of

[
MM92
, Theorem VI.6.1]
.
∎

The following result is probably folkloric, but its subsequent utility suggests
that a proof is appropriate. This allows element-wise checks of internal subset
containments and will be used in many arguments.

Corollary 3.7
.

For arrows
f,g\colon W\rightrightarrows PX
, we have

W\Vdash f\leq g\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash\forall x((x\in_{X}f)\Rightarrow_{\Omega}(x\in_{X}g))

Proof.

Using the transpose isomorphism
Equation

2.4
,
we have

W\Vdash f\leq g\text{ if, and only if, }X\times W\Vdash(1_{X}\in_{X}f)\leq(1_{X}\in_{X}g)

in the order on
\Omega
. But since the implication operator on
\Omega
is
the classifying arrow of the order, we thus have

W\Vdash f\leq g\text{ if, and only if, }X\times W\Vdash((1_{X}\in_{X}f)\Rightarrow_{\Omega}(1_{X}\in_{X}g))

which now implies the conclusion by the implication and universal
quantification clauses of
Theorem

3.6
.
∎

As a first application, we have the Kripke-Joyal semantics of the interior
operator (
Definition

3.1
).

Proposition 3.8

(Forcing Semantics of Interior)
.

If
(X,r)
is a relation, then

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash\forall y((a\leq y)\Rightarrow_{\Omega}(y\in_{X}s))

for any elements
\langle a,s\rangle\colon W\rightrightarrows X\times PX
.

Proof.

By construction,
\mathsf{int}\colon PX\to PX
is the transpose of
\uparrow\leq 1
since it occurs in the diagram

Consequently, we have a characterization

(x\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s))=(\uparrow\!\!x\leq_{PX}s)

for any
\langle x,s\rangle\colon W\to X\times PX
. Accordingly, we have
the equivalence

W\Vdash(x\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s))\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash(\uparrow\!\!x\leq_{PX}s).

The desired result now follows by the element-wise characterization of subset
containment in
Corollary

3.7
and the
construction of
\uparrow\colon X\to PX
.
∎

Our next task is to give analogous semantics of the closure operator. Since the
closure is a composite of an internal join and an internal direct image, we
shall study the semantics of each in turn. The result for direct image is
essentially that any
a
in
\exists_{f}s
is of the form
a=fb
for some
b
in

s
, as is true in ordinary set theory.

Proposition 3.9
.

For any morphism
f\colon A\to B
, we have

W\Vdash a\in_{B}\exists_{f}(s)\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash\exists y((a=_{B}fy)\wedge(y\in_{A}s))

for any
\langle a,s\rangle\colon W\to A\times PA
.

Proof.

For the forward direction, assume that
W\Vdash a\in_{B}\exists_{f}(s)
. From the direct image construction in
Section

2.2
, this means equivalently that
W\Vdash\chi\langle a,s\rangle
where
\chi
is the classifying morphism of the image of the composite
U_{X}\to A\times PA\to B\times PA
in the construction. Thus,
\langle a,s\rangle
factors through that image via a morphism
w
as in the diagram:
