\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

This diagram also depicts
e
from the construction of
\exists_{f}
and its
pullback
p
along
w
given by the dashed arrows. Note that
p
is an
epimorphism since
e
is one. Now, since by construction
me
factors as

me=(f\times 1)\langle b,a\rangle
, we have two equations, namely,

ap=fbq\text{ and }sp=aq.

The first of these implies that
V\Vdash ap=_{B}fbq
holds as a statement of
internal equality. The second, along with the computation

\chi\langle a,s\rangle p=\chi meq=(bq\in_{A}aq)=(bq\in_{A}sp)

implies that
V\Vdash bq\in_{A}sp
too. Putting the two forcing statements
together as a conjunction and then existentially quantifying, we get that
the desired forcing statement
W\Vdash\exists y((a=_{B}fy)\wedge(y\in_{A}s))

by
Theorem

3.6
since
p
is an epimorphism. We leave
the converse to the reader’s ingenuity since all the steps in the forward
direction are essentially reversible.
∎

Now, having characterized when an element is in a direct image, an examination
of the semantics of the internal join makes good on the promise of

Remark

3.3
. As a reminder, we would like to
see that membership in a join of a family of subsets is witnessed by membership
in a particular element of that family. The next result shows that this is
indeed the case. Note that the proof bears some formal similarity to the proof
of the previous result. Throughout we use the notation of

Construction

3.1
.

Proposition 3.10

(Forcing Semantics of Join)
.

Let
X
be any object. The join operator
\bigvee\colon P^{2}X\to PX
then satisfies

W\Vdash x\in_{X}\bigvee s\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash\exists y(x\in_{X}y\wedge y\in_{PX}s)

for any element
\langle x,s\rangle\colon W\to X\times P^{2}X
. In other words,
x
is in the join if, and only if, there is a member
y
of the collection of which
x
is a member.

Proof.

We shall prove the forward direction. Assume that
W\Vdash x\in_{PX}\bigvee s
holds. Equivalently, then,
W\Vdash\top_{W}\in_{\Omega}\hat{\phi}(x,s)
and
indeed
W\Vdash\phi\langle\top_{W},\langle x,s\rangle\rangle
both hold by
the equivalence above in

Equation

3.3
and
then the construction of
\hat{\phi}
. The last forcing statement implies
that
\langle\top_{W},\langle x,s\rangle\rangle
factors through
I
via
some arrow, say,
w\colon W\to I
. Consider now the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times U_{PX}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\times X\times P^{2}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\top_{W},\langle x,s\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

whose dashed arrows realize
V
as the corner object of the pullback of
e

along
w
. Note that
p
is epi. Since by construction
me
factors
\langle 1\in c,1\times d\rangle
, we have

\langle\top_{V},\langle xp,sp\rangle\rangle=\langle(1\in c)q,(1\times d)q\rangle

hence writing
q=\langle q_{1},q_{2}\rangle
and comparing arguments in the
above equation, we get three further equations

\top_{V}=q_{1}\in_{X}cq_{1}\text{ and }xp=q_{1}\text{ and }sp=dq_{2}.

Consequently, putting together the first two, we have

V\Vdash xp\in_{X}cq_{2}

and from the commutative diagram given by the third equation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{PX}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{PX}\times W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{PX\times P^{2}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle c,d\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle q_{2},p\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle b,s\rangle}

we have finally

V\Vdash cq_{2}\in_{PX}sp.

By the forcing semantics for
\wedge
summarized in

Theorem

3.6
, these last two forcing statements are
equivalent to

V\Vdash(xp\in_{X}cq_{2}\wedge cq_{2}\in_{PX}sp)

hence to

W\Vdash\exists y(x\in_{X}y\wedge y\in_{PX}s)

by the forcing semantics for
\exists
in
Theorem

3.6

since
p
is an epimorphism. This completes the proof of the forward
implication. The conscientious reader can reverse the implications to complete the proof of the converse.
∎

Corollary 3.11

(Forcing Semantics of Closure)
.

If
(X,r)
is any relation, then

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(s)\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash\exists y((a\leq y)\wedge(y\in_{X}s))

for any element
\langle a,s\rangle\colon W\to X\times PX
.

Proof.

The left side of the equivalence is characterized as:

\displaystyle W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(s)

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash\exists y(a\in_{X}y\wedge y\in_{PX}\exists_{\downarrow}(s))

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }V\Vdash ap\in_{X}b\text{ and }V\Vdash b\in_{PX}\exists_{\downarrow}(sp)

for an epimorphism
p\colon V\to W
and element
b\colon V\to PX
by the
previous two propositions. The right conjunct is another direct image and so
we have:

\displaystyle V\Vdash b\in_{PX}\exists_{\downarrow}(sp)

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }V\Vdash\exists y((b=\downarrow\!\!y)\wedge y\in_{X}sp))

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }U\Vdash bq=\downarrow\!\!c\text{ and }U\Vdash c\in_{X}spq

for some epimorphism
q\colon U\to V
and element
c\colon U\to V
. On the
other hand, the left conjunct can be characterized as

\displaystyle V\Vdash ap\in_{X}b

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }U\Vdash apq\in_{X}bq

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }U\Vdash apq\in_{X}\downarrow\!\!c

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }U\Vdash apq\leq c

by the fact that
q
is epi, hence right cancellable, and the computation
above showing that
bq
is the principal downset on
c
. The two resulting
forcing statments
U\Vdash c\in_{X}spq
and
U\Vdash apq\leq c
combine to
yield
W\Vdash\exists y(a\leq y\wedge y\in s)
by

Theorem

3.6
, yielding the right side of the desired
equivalence. This proves the forward direction. The reverse direction is a
similar argument, unwinding the definitions and using the two propositions
and Kripke-Joyal semantics.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

The equivalences characterizing membership in interiors and closures in

Proposition

3.8
and

Corollary

3.11
are
formal
in the sense
that they each incorporate internal logical operators and quantification.
However, in the subsequent calculations and applications, we would prefer
to argue in a more natural or intuitive way, ignoring where possible the
somewhat demanding formalism of these characterizations. Kripke-Joyal
semantics makes this possible. For example, owing to existential and
conjunction clauses of
Theorem

3.6
, we have that

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(s)\text{ if, and only if, }V\Vdash(ap\leq b)\text{ and }V\Vdash(b\in_{X}sp))

for some epimorphism
p\colon V\to W
and element
b\colon V\to X
. We allow
ourselves to write

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(s)\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash(a\leq b)\text{ and }W\Vdash(b\in_{X}s)\text{ for some $b$}

with the understanding that technically we should insert the epimorphism

p
and that it can be done in principle, but will be ignored unless it
matters in the computation at hand. Likewise, we have

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash(a\leq b)\text{ implies }W\Vdash(b\in_{X}s)\text{ for all $b$}

for interior semantics. Similarly, we will argue informally in the
case of internal inequalities and element-wise subset containments as
stated formally in
Corollary

3.7
.
∎

3.3
Validating the (IS4) Axioms

This section has two goals. The first is to show that if
(X,r)
is an internal
order, then
PX
with the operators
\mathsf{int}
and
\mathsf{cl}
is a categorical
model of (IS4) in the sense of
Definition

2.11
. Second is to
show that if
(X,r)
is an equivalence relation, then the model is an MAO-couple
as in
Definition

2.13
.
Wherever possible we will reason informally about interior and closure as in

Remark

3.12
.

Throughout let
(X,r)
denote a relation
r
on
X
in a topos
\mathscr{E}
. The
morphism
\mathsf{int}:=P(\uparrow)\downarrow\colon PX\to PX
from

Definition

3.1
will yield our semantics of the
modal
\Box
-operator. Likewise
\mathsf{cl}:=\bigvee\exists_{\downarrow}\colon PX\to PX
of
Definition

3.4
will be our semantics of
the
\Diamond
-operator.

Lemma 3.13
.

The operators
\mathsf{int},\mathsf{cl}\colon PX\rightrightarrows PX
are
order-preserving, hence internal functors.

Proof.

Certainly
\mathsf{int}
is one since it is a composite of functors. Likewise,
by
[
Joh14
, Proposition 5.36]
, the join is order-preserving, implying that
\mathsf{cl}
is a functor as well.
∎

First we shall show that
\mathsf{int}
is a comonad on
PX
. In fact it will be
cartesian monoidal since
PX
is preordered. Throughout we shall
perform element-wise checks of containments using the result of
Corollary

3.7
. In particular, to show the counit
condition, namely, that
\mathsf{int}\leq 1_{PX}
holds, it suffices to prove that

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)\text{ implies }W\Vdash a\in_{X}s

holds for any
a\colon W\to X
. That this indeed is the case is the content of
the proof of the first result.

Lemma 3.14
.

If the relation
(X,r)
is reflexive, then
\mathsf{int}\colon PX\to PX

satisfies the counit law
\mathsf{int}\leq 1_{PX}
.

Proof.

Suppose then that
W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)
holds; we shall prove that

W\Vdash a\in_{X}s
holds. But the hypothesis unpacks as the valid implication

W\Vdash a\leq_{r}b\text{ implies }W\Vdash b\in_{X}s

for any
b\colon W\to X
. But by reflexivity,
W\Vdash a\leq_{r}a
always
holds. This implies by the statement immediately above that

W\Vdash a\in_{X}s
as required.
∎

Now, to show the comultiplication law, we want to show that the inequality

\mathsf{int}\leq\mathsf{int}\,\mathsf{int}
holds. This amounts to showing that

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)\text{ implies }W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{int}\,\mathsf{int}(s)

(3.8)

for any generalized element
\langle a,s\rangle\colon W\to X\times PX
. We can
make a couple of reductions before embarking upon the proof. The consequent of
the desired statement

Equation

3.8
is equivalent to

W\Vdash a\leq b\text{ implies }W\Vdash b\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)

(3.9)

for any element
b\colon W\to X
. Thus, we will assume the antecedents of

Equation

3.8
and

Equation

3.9
and show the above
consequent, namely,
W\Vdash b\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)
.

Proposition 3.15
.

If the relation
(X,r)
is an order, then
\mathsf{int}
satisfies the
comultiplication law
\mathsf{int}\leq\mathsf{int}\,\mathsf{int}
. In particular

\mathsf{int}
is a cartesian monoidal comonad on
PX
.

Proof.

As discussed above, assume that
W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)
and

W\Vdash a\leq b
both hold for appropriate generalized elements. The first
assumption yields the equivalence:

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash a\leq c\text{ implies }W\Vdash c\in_{X}s

for any element
c\colon W\to X
. The desired statement is formally analogous:

W\Vdash b\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash b\leq d\text{ implies }W\Vdash d\in_{X}s

for any
d\colon W\to X
. We can prove the right side of the equivalence
immediately above. For if
W\Vdash b\leq d
for such
d
, then since

W\Vdash a\leq b
by transitivity we conclude that
W\Vdash a\leq d
too.
Therefore, since
a
is an interior point,
W\Vdash d\in_{X}s
holds. For
product-preservation, it suffices to show that
\downarrow\colon PX\to P^{2}X

is product-preserving since
P(\uparrow)
is a right adjoint. But

\downarrow\colon PX\to P^{2}X
is also a right adjoint since
PX
is an
internal sup-lattice. The coherence axioms for a monoidal comonad are satisfied automatically since
PX
is an internal preorder.
∎

Now, we shall show that closure is a monad. First we shall check the unit axiom,
namely, that
1\leq\mathsf{cl}
holds. By
Corollary

3.7
,
this is equivalent to showing the implication

W\Vdash a\in_{X}s\text{ implies }W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(s)

for any element
\langle a,s\rangle\colon W\to X\times PX
.

Lemma 3.16
.

If
(X,r)
is a reflexive relation on
X
, then
1\leq\mathsf{cl}
holds.

Proof.

Let
\langle a,s\rangle\colon W\to X\times PX
denote any element.
Suppose that
W\Vdash a\in_{X}s
. By reflexivity
W\Vdash a\leq a

certainly holds, which implies that
W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(s)
by closure
semantics.
∎

Transitivity implies the multiplication law. It suffices to prove the implication

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}\,\mathsf{cl}(s)\text{ implies }W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(s)

for any element
\langle a,s\rangle\colon W\to X\times PX
.

Lemma 3.17
.

If
(X,r)
is transitive, then
\mathsf{cl}\,\mathsf{cl}\leq\mathsf{cl}
holds.
Consequently,
\mathsf{cl}
is a monad on
PX
if
X
is an order.

Proof.

Assume the antecedent of the implication in the above display. This yields
the equivalence

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}\,\mathsf{cl}(s)\text{ if, and only if, }W\Vdash a\leq b\text{ and }W\Vdash b\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(s)

and of course the rightmost conjunct is equivalent to
the conjunction of
W\Vdash b\leq c
and
W\Vdash c\in s
. But then by
transitivity,
W\Vdash a\leq c
holds, meaning we can conclude that

W\Vdash(a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(s))
holds too, proving the implication.
∎

We now have our first main result.

Theorem 3.18
.

If
(X,r)
is an order on
X
, then
PX

with
\mathsf{int},\mathsf{cl}\colon PX\rightrightarrows PX
is a categorical
order model of (IS4) as in
Definition

2.11
.

Proof.

Any power object
PX
is an internal Heyting algebra, hence in particular
internally cartesian closed and cocomplete. By

Proposition

3.15
and

Lemma

3.17
, it therefore only remains only to see that

\mathsf{cl}
is
\mathsf{int}
-strong. By

Corollary

3.7
, it suffices to prove that

W\Vdash(a\in_{X}(\mathsf{int}(s)\wedge\mathsf{cl}(u)))\text{ implies }W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(\mathsf{int}(s)\wedge u)

for any
a\colon W\to X
and
s,u\colon W\rightrightarrows PX
. But this
follows by transtivity. Supposing the antecendent, we need to produce a
b

such that

W\Vdash a\leq b\text{ and }W\Vdash b\in_{X}(\mathsf{int}(s)\wedge u).

But such a
b
is given by the antecedent hypothesis that

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{cl}(u)
. For from this assumption
W\Vdash b\in u

holds for some
b
. It needs only to be seen that
W\Vdash b\in\mathsf{int}(s)
. So suppose that
c
satisfies
W\Vdash b\leq c
. But then by
transitivity
W\Vdash a\leq c
, meaning that
W\vdash c\in_{X}s
since

W\Vdash a\in_{X}\mathsf{int}(s)
by assumption.
∎

Our final goal is to show that, supposing symmetry, interior and closure make

PX
into an MAO-couple (
Definition

2.13
). By

Theorem

3.18
,this requires showing only that

1.

\mathsf{int}
and
\mathsf{cl}
are idempotent;

2.

\mathsf{int}
and
\mathsf{cl}
form an adjoint modality with

\mathsf{cl}\dashv\mathsf{int}
.

Idempotence is immediate as a formal consequence of the internal preorder on

PX
. So, we shall focus on adjointness. Internally, this is the statement that

(1\leq_{PX}\mathsf{int}\,\mathsf{cl})\text{ and }(\mathsf{cl}\,\mathsf{int}\leq_{PX}1)

(3.10)

are both both valid, that is, equal to
\top_{PX}
. That this is the case can
be shown purely from the calculus of internal adjoints. First we need a
preliminary lemma, explaining the connection between symmetry and the direct
image of the downset operator.

Lemma 3.19
.

If
(X,r)
is a symmetric order, then the upset and downset morphisms are
equal:
\uparrow=\downarrow
.

Proof.

The upper- and lower-set morphisms are constructed as transposes as in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times PX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times PX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\uparrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\downarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq_{r}}

where the isomorphism on the right exchanges the factors in the product of
X
with itself. But symmetry means that there is a morphism
R\to R
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes. But the square is a pullback, which implies that the two arrows
X\times X\to\Omega
in the first display are equal by uniqueness of classifying arrows, meaning that their transposes are also equal.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

The previous lemma justifies the following notation. When
(X,r)
is an
equivalence relation, the classifying arrow of
r\colon R\to X\times X
will
be denoted by
\approx\colon X\times X\to\Omega
. The transpose of
\approx

will be denoted by
[-]\colon X\to PX
, thought of as taking
x\in X
to its
equivalence class
[x]=\{y\in X\mid x\approx y\}
. ∎

Theorem 3.21
.

If
(X,r)
is an internal equivalence relation, then the closure operator

\mathsf{cl}\colon PX\to PX
and interior operator
\mathsf{int}\colon PX\to PX

are internally adjoint:
\mathsf{cl}\dashv\mathsf{int}
. Consequently, interior
and closure make the power object
PX
into an MAO-couple
(
Definition

2.13
).

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.19
, we shall write

[-]
for the downset and upset morphisms since they are equal. The two
identities that need to be shown are the unit and counit laws, namely, that
both inequalities

1\leq\mathsf{int}\,\mathsf{cl}\text{ and }\mathsf{cl}\,\mathsf{int}\leq 1

are valid, that is, equal to
\top_{PX}
. Each verification is straighforward
by the internal calculus of adjoints. On the one hand, for the conunit
equation, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{cl}\,\mathsf{int}\leq 1

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }\bigvee\exists_{[-]}\mathsf{int}\leq 1\qquad

(def.
\mathsf{cl}
in
Definition

3.4
)

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }\exists_{[-]}\mathsf{int}\leq[-]\qquad

(
\bigvee\dashv[-]
)

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }\mathsf{int}\leq P([-])[-]\qquad

(
\exists_{[-]}\dashv P([-])
)

Since the last inequality is clearly valid by the definition of
\mathsf{int}

in
Definition

3.1
, this proves the counit
inequality. Notice that symmetry is used here in the last step since

\mathsf{int}
has both up- and down-set operators in its definition, but these
are equal by the lemma. The other required inequality has a similar proof.
∎

3.4
The Obviously Undivided Relation

With the main theorem proved, we can now return to the application of the
histories presheaf from the end of
Section

2.3
. Again let
\mathscr{W}
denote the poset
our world
and let
H\colon\mathscr{W}^{op}\to\mathbf{Set}
denote the histories presheaf sending a point event
e
to the set
H_{e}
of histories containing it. First we shall define the relation
obviously undivided
at
e
leading to our model.

Definition 3.22
.

Two histories
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
containing a point event
e
are

obviously undivided at

e
if both
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
contain some
further point event
e^{\prime}
strictly later than
e
, in that
e\leq e^{\prime}
and

e\neq e^{\prime}
, if there are any such point events. Otherwise
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
,
provided they each contain
e
,
apparently diverge
at
e
. Write

h_{1}\approx_{e}h_{2}
when
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are obviously undivided at the
common point event
e
.∎

Example 3.23
.

Return to the situation of
Example

2.19

where
\mathscr{W}
consists of the four point events and three nontrivial arrows
as in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{:=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

This example, however simple, captures some of the fundamental ideas of
branching and the significance of
choice points
, namely, those
point events
e
which are maximal in the intersection of two histories

[
Bel92
, Fact 25, p.407]
. In this example,
e_{0}
is the unique
choice point. Since
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
contain
e_{-1}
and
e_{0}
is properly
later than
e_{-1}
, we have that
h_{1}\approx_{e_{-1}}h_{2}
, i.e.,
h_{1}
and

h_{2}
are obviously undivided at
e_{-1}
. Roughly, events around any choice
point in a
real
branching space-time would intuitively look something
like
\mathscr{W}
. So, we think of this example as the smallest model of
non-trivial branching and obvious undividedness.
∎

Notice that if
e
is a maximal point event in our world histories
h_{1}
and

h_{2}
containing such
e
are obviously undivided at
e
by vacuity. Thus,

\approx_{e}
is reflexive. It is also trivially symmetric. Two further assumptions
on our world make the relation
\approx_{e}
into a particularly nice one. First
is the assumption of
existence of infima
: each nonempty chain that is
bounded below has an infimum in each history of which it is a subset. Secondly,
there is the assumption of
density
, namely, that given
e\leq e^{\prime}
there
is a point event properly between
e
and
e^{\prime}
. Both for example are true of
Minkowski space-time.

Lemma 3.24
.

Assume that our world satisfies the infima and density assumptions summarized above. For each point event
e
of our world,
\approx_{e}
is then an equivalence relation on
H_{e}
.

Proof.

As remarked above, evidently
\approx_{e}
is reflexive and symmetric. It has
only to be seen that it is transitive. The proof of Fact 46 in

[
Bel92
, §A.2.]
shows this given the assumptions of density and
existence of infima.
∎

The final task in this section is that of showing that the pointwise-defined

obviously undivided
relation induces an equivalence relation
(
Definition

2.5
) on the histories presheaf of

Lemma

2.18
. This will follow by a lemma showing that
equivalence relations in a presheaf topos can be defined pointwise.

Lemma 3.25
.

A relation
R\to P\times P
in a presheaf category
[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}]
is
an equivalence relation if, and only if,
Rc\to Pc\times Pc
is one in

\mathbf{Set}
for each
c\in\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

The forward direction is a triviality. For the converse, suppose that each
Rc\to Pc\times Pc
is an equivalence relation. The proofs consist merely in showing that any induced morphisms factoring through
Rc
comprise a natural family. For symmetry this is immediate. The arguments for reflexivity and transitivity are identical in strategy. For example, instantiating transitivity, for each object
c
, the corner object
Tc
factors through
Rc
as in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tc}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rc}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Pc\times Pc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle ap,bq\rangle_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle a,b\rangle_{c}}

These morphisms
\psi_{c}
comprise a natural transformation. For each
\langle a,b\rangle_{c}
is monic, i.e. injective in
\mathbf{Set}
. So, for a morphism
f\colon c\to d
, we calculate that

\langle a,b\rangle_{c}R(f)\psi_{d}=(Pf\times Pf)\langle ap,bq\rangle=\langle a,b\rangle_{c}\psi_{c}Tf

which implies the naturality equation
R(f)\psi_{d}=\psi_{c}Tf
by cancelling
\langle a,b\rangle_{c}
. Again the same strategy works to show reflexivity.
∎

Theorem 3.26
.

The obviously undivided relation induces an equivalence relation on the
histories presheaf. Therefore, the histories presheaf
H
canonically
induces an MAO-couple in the presheaf topos on our world.

Proof.

It suffices by
Lemma

3.25
to do
a pointwise check. But this is confirmed by

Lemma

3.24
which showed that
each
\approx_{e}
is an equivalence relation on the set
H_{e}
of histories
containing
e
. The model statement follows now just by noting

Theorem

3.21
.
∎

4
Applications, Interpretations and Computations

This section develops illustrations, elaborations, and examples of the previous
results. In
Section

4.1
, we carry out a
number of computations in presheaf categories that illustrate the abstractions
more concretely. These are applied in
Section

4.2
to
discuss the so-called
indistinguishability interpretation
of equivalence
relations providing the natural models for
epistemic logic
. We shall see
that forcing statements arising from the obviously undivided relation have a
natural interpretation in this light. Presheaf computations are also applied in

Section

4.3
in the investigation of several
first-order modal formulas, including the Barcan formulas, existence
predicates, and then the validity of the actualist thesis

Equation

A
.

4.1
Presheaf Category Computations

This subsection is devoted to unwinding our general constructions for presheaf
toposes
\mathscr{E}=[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}]
. The reference for generalities of such
toposes is
[
MM92
]
. We adopt the convention that the action
of the transition function
X(f)\colon XC\to XD
for an arrow
f\colon D\to C

and element
a\in XC
will be denoted by
a\cdot f:=X(f)(a)
.

Our goal is to understand the interior operator in this special case. It is
tempting to jump to the conclusion that the forcing semantics of necessity
statements
\Box\phi\colon X\to\Omega
is given by

C\Vdash a\in_{X}\Box\phi\text{ if, and only if, }C\Vdash a\leq b\text{ implies }C\Vdash\phi_{C}(b).

This looks plausible given
Proposition

3.8
but needs to be
proved. This will be done in detail over the course of the subsection. The resulting proposition will be used throughout
Section

4.3
.

First recall
[
MM92
, §VI.7]
that, since every presheaf is
a colimit of representables and that transformations
\alpha\colon\mathbf{y}C\to X
are essentially elements
a\in XC
, the forcing relation takes the form

C\Vdash\phi(a)\text{ if, and only if, }\phi_{C}(a)=\top_{X}\text{ if, and only if, }a\in\{x\mid\phi\}(C)

(4.1)

where
\{x\mid\phi\}
denotes the subobject classified by

\phi\colon X\to\Omega
. In the following proposition, the morphism

\Box\phi\colon 1\to PX
is the composite of
\Box:=\mathsf{int}\colon PX\to PX

and the transpose
\hat{\phi}\colon 1\to PX
of
\phi\colon X\to\Omega
in the
presheaf topos
\mathscr{E}=[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}]
. Likewise a similar pattern
yields the morphism
\Diamond\phi
. Forcing statements are most distinctive for

\Omega
-valued morphisms that are not necessarily closed formulas, so we
consider the transposes
(1_{X}\in_{C}\Box\phi)\colon X\to\Omega
and
(1_{X}\in_{C}\Diamond\phi)\colon X\to\Omega
.

Proposition 4.1
.

For any proposition
\phi\colon X\to\Omega
where
(X,r)
is a relation,

1.

C\Vdash(a\in_{C}\Box\phi)
if, and only if, for all
b\in XC
with
a\leq_{C}b
it follows that
C\Vdash\phi_{C}(b)

2.

C\Vdash(a\in_{C}\Diamond\phi)
if, and only if, there is
b\in XC
with
a\leq_{C}b
with
C\Vdash\phi_{C}(b)
.

Proof.

These statements result from instantiating

Proposition

3.8
and

Corollary

3.11
using the observation above that
elements
\mathbf{y}C\to X
are in bijection with elements of
XC
and that
the
C
-component of
b\in_{X}\hat{\phi}
is equal to
\phi_{C}(b)
by
transposition. A detailed examination of the necessity operator amounting
to a direct proof of the first clause of the proposition appears in

Example

4.7
.
∎

The reader comfortable with the reference, or at least the constructions
therein, can skip most of the following generalities and look at

Example

4.2
,

Example

4.5
and

Example

4.7
which are the main novelties
of the subsection. These are a complete description of the upset and downset
arrows
\uparrow,\downarrow\colon X\rightrightarrows PX
in any presheaf topos,
as well as a more in-depth analysis of the interior operator and its semantics.

Construction 4.1

(Subobject Classifier)
.

Let
\Omega\colon\mathscr{C}^{op}\to\mathbf{Set}
denote the presheaf given by

\Omega(C)=\{S\mid S\text{ is a sieve on }C\}

on objects. On a given arrow
f\colon C\to D
, the associated function
\Omega(f)\colon\Omega(D)\to\Omega(C)
makes the assignment

\Omega(f)(T)=f^{*}T=\{g\mid fg\in T\}

for any sieve
T
on
D
. It is straighforward to check that this is a
sieve and that
\Omega
so defined is a presheaf. Given a monomorphism

S\to X
in the presheaf topos, the associated classifying morphism

\chi\colon X\to\Omega
has components
\chi_{C}\colon X(C)\to\Omega(C)

given by

\chi_{C}(a)=\{f\colon D\to C\mid a\cdot f\in S(D)\}.

(4.2)

That is,
\chi_{C}(a)
is the sieve of morphisms
f
into
C
whose action
under
Xf
ends up in the appropriate component of the subobject
S
. The
top element of
\Omega
is
\top\colon 1\to\Omega
picking out the

total sieve
of all arrows on each object
C
. It is now
straighforward to verify that for each subobject of
X
, the arrow
\chi

is the unique morphism into
\Omega
realizing the subobject as a pullback
of
\top
along
\chi
. ∎

Example 4.2

(Classifying Morphisms for Orders)
.

Let
(X,r)
denote a relation
r\colon R\to X\times X
on a presheaf
X
.
This is point-wise a relation
r_{C}\colon RC\to XC\times XC
in
\mathbf{Set}
for
each object
C
. The classifying morphism
\leq_{r}\colon X\times X\to\Omega
fits into a pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}

and by the description in

Equation

4.2
, for any object
C
,
we have

a\leq_{C}b=\{f\colon D\to C\mid(a\cdot f\leq_{D}b\cdot f)\}.

(4.3)

Note that here we have written
\leq
for
\leq_{r}
and preferred the infix
notation since it is easier to parse. So, in other words, the image of

(a,b)
under the classifying arrow is the sieve of arrows
f\colon D\to C

under whose action
a
and
b
are related under
r
in the sense that

a\cdot f\leq b\cdot f
in
X(D)
. We shall be interested in this
construction when
(X,r)
is at least an order, but especially when it is
an equivalence relation.∎

Example 4.3

(Kernel of a Morphism)
.

Let
\tau\colon X\to Y
denote any morphism in a topos
\mathscr{E}
. The

kernel
of
\tau
is the pullback of
\tau
along itself

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times_{Y}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

and the projections always yield an equivalence relation

\langle\lambda,\rho\rangle\colon X\times_{Y}X\to X\times X
. In the case
when
\mathscr{E}=[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}]
, since limits are computed pointwise
in
\mathbf{Set}
, for each
C\in\mathscr{C}
, we have a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{XC\times_{YC}XC}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces XC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces XC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces YC}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

whose corner object consists of those pairs
(a,b)
satisfying

\tau_{C}a=\tau_{C}b
. Accordingly, the classifying morphism

\ker(\tau)\colon X\times X\to\Omega
has components
\ker(\tau)_{C}
whose
action is

\ker(\tau)_{C}(a,b)=\{f\colon D\to C\mid\tau_{D}(a\cdot f)=\tau_{D}(b\cdot f)\}

that is, whose action produces the sieve of arrows into
C
whose action on

a
and
b
are equalized by the appropriate component of
\tau
. ∎

Example 4.4

(Identity Relation)
.

In any topos, the classifier of the diagonal morphism on an object
X

serves as an identity predicate
=_{X}\colon X\times X\to\Omega
.
When
X
is a presheaf, each component of the identity predicate

(=_{X})_{C}\colon XC\times XC\to\Omega C
has the action

a(=_{X})_{C}b=\{f\colon D\to C\mid a\cdot f=b\cdot f\}\qquad a,b\in XC.

The equation
a\cdot f=b\cdot f
is set-theoretic equality in
X(D)

since each component of
\Delta\colon X\to X\times X
is the actual
set-theoretic diagonal. So, colloquially,
a(=_{X})_{C}b
is the sieve of
those arrows into
C
under which the action on
a
and
b
are literally
equal in
XD
. ∎

The next task is to look carefully at transposes and in particular at those of
the classifying morphisms of orders as above. This is so we can understand the
upset and downset arrows from
Definition

2.6
in this
context. Generally, exponential objects

Q^{P}
are given by a homset formula, namely,

Q^{P}(C)=[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}](\mathbf{y}C\times P,Q)

(4.4)

that is, as the set of all transformations
\mathbf{y}C\times P\to Q
. For a
morphism
f\colon D\to C
, the action of
Q^{P}(f)
is to take a transformation

\theta\colon\mathbf{y}C\times P\to Q
to one

Q^{P}(f)(\theta)\colon\mathbf{y}D\times P\to Q
defined by

\mathscr{C}(B,D)\times PB\to QB\qquad(g,b)\mapsto\theta_{B}(fg,b)

(4.5)

where
g\colon B\to D
any arrow and
b\in PB
is any element. Since power objects
can be viewed as special exponentials, namely,
PX=\Omega^{X}
, we can use this
construction to do our calculations.∎

Construction 4.2

(Power Objects)
.

For any presheaf
X\colon\mathscr{C}^{op}\to\mathbf{Set}
, the associated power object is the exponential
PX=\Omega^{X}
whose value at any object
C
is

PX(C)=\Omega^{X}(C)=[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}](\mathbf{y}C\times X,\Omega).

That is,
PX(C)
is the set of transformations
\mathbf{y}C\times X\to\Omega
. Any such
\tau\colon\mathbf{y}C\times X\to\Omega
has components

\tau_{D}\colon\mathscr{C}(D,C)\times X(D)\to\Omega(D)\qquad\qquad\tau_{D}(f,a)\in\Omega(D).

picking out
\tau_{D}(f,a)
, a sieve on
D
, for each such pair
(f,a)
consisting of an arrow
f\colon D\to C
and an element
a\in X(D)
. Given a transformation
\tau\colon X\times Y\to\Omega
, the transpose is the unique transformation
\hat{g}\colon Y\to PX
making

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times PX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\hat{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

commute and for each object
C
the component
\hat{g}_{C}\colon Y(C)\to PX(C)
has the action of sending
b\in Y(C)
to a transformation
\mathbf{y}C\times X\to\Omega
given by

\hat{g}_{C}(b)_{D}\colon\mathscr{C}(D,C)\times X(D)\to\Omega D\qquad\hat{g}_{C}(b)_{D}(f,a)=g(a,Yf(b))

for any object
D
of
\mathscr{C}
. In the special case of a transformation

\phi\colon X\to\Omega
, we can compute the transpose
\hat{\phi}\colon 1\to PX
from this general formula. The transpose is the unique such morphism
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times PX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times 1\cong X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\hat{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

commute. For any object
C
, the set function
\hat{\phi}_{C}\colon 1\to PX(C)

picks out a distinguished transformation
\hat{\phi}_{C}(\ast)\colon\mathbf{y}C\times X\to\Omega
whose components are described by

\hat{\phi}_{C}(\ast)_{D}\colon\mathscr{C}(D,C)\times X(D)\to\Omega(D)\qquad\hat{\phi}_{C}(\ast)_{D}(f,a)=\phi_{D}(a).

(4.6)

Note that the formula on the right ignores the arrow
f
and evaluates the
appropriate component of
\phi
at the element
a\in X(D)
. ∎

Example 4.5

(Upset and Downset Arrows)
.

Continue with the elaboration begun in

Example

4.2
. Let
(X,r)

denote any relation in
[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}]
. The downset arrow

\downarrow\colon X\to PX
has components
\downarrow_{C}\colon XC\to PX(C)

sending each
b\in XC
to a transformation
\mathbf{y}C\times X\Rightarrow\Omega
whose components over
D\in\mathscr{C}
are described by the action

\downarrow\!\!_{C}(b)_{D}(f,a)=a\leq_{D}b\cdot f\qquad(f,a)\in\mathscr{C}(D,C)\times XD.

(4.7)

So, in other words, using the description of

Equation

4.3
, we have that

\downarrow\!\!_{C}(b)_{D}(f,a)
is the sieve of those arrows
g\colon B\to D
for
which
a\cdot g\leq_{B}b\cdot fg
holds. Likewise, the upset arrow

\uparrow\colon X\to PX
has the action

\uparrow\!\!_{C}(b)_{D}(f,a)=b\cdot f\leq_{D}a\qquad(f,a)\in\mathscr{C}(D,C)\times XD.

(4.8)

So, specifically, this action amounts to the sieve of those
g\colon B\to D
for which
b\cdot fg\leq_{B}a\cdot g
holds, effectively just
reversing that of
\downarrow
. We will later apply the downset arrow
construction to the case of
PX
with its order induced from
\Omega
. In
this case
\downarrow\colon PX\to P^{2}X
has components
\downarrow_{C}

taking a transformation
\theta\colon\mathbf{y}C\times X\Rightarrow\Omega

to one
\downarrow\!\!_{C}(\theta)\colon\mathbf{y}C\times PX\Rightarrow\Omega

components

\theta_{C}(\theta)_{D}\colon\mathscr{C}(D,C)\times PX(C)\to\Omega(C)

with action

\downarrow\!\!_{C}(\theta)_{D}(f,\tau):=(\tau\leq\theta\cdot f).

(4.9)

Here the action
\theta\cdot f
is essentially precomposition with
f
.
That is, the transformation
\theta\cdot f
is the composite

\theta(f-,-)\colon\mathbf{y}D\times X\Rightarrow\Omega
having the action

(\theta\cdot f)_{B}(g,b)=\theta_{B}(fg,b)

where
(\theta\cdot f)_{B}\colon\mathscr{C}(B,D)\times XB\to\Omega(B)
. Thus,
more precisely,
\tau\leq\theta\cdot f
from the computation in

Equation

4.9
is the sieve of arrows

g\colon B\to D
for which
\tau\cdot f\leq\theta\cdot fg
holds where
the action is described as above. ∎

Example 4.6

(Singleton Morphism)
.

In the special case of the previous example where
r\colon R\to X\times X

is the diagonal
\Delta\colon X\to X\times X
and
\leq_{r}
is the identity
predicate
=_{X}
, the upset (and downset) morphisms are traditionally denote
by
\sigma_{X}\colon X\to PX
, thought of as the singleton morphism

x\mapsto\{x\}
. In this case, the action is

\sigma_{X}(b)_{D}(f,a)=(a=_{X}b\cdot f)

using the description of
Equation

4.7
. Of
course this result is a sieve, namely, the sieve of arrows
g\colon B\to D

for which
a\cdot g=b\cdot fg
holds as a literal equality in
XB
. ∎

Construction 4.3
.

Fix a relation
(X,r)
in a presheaf topos
\mathscr{E}=[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}]

and let
\uparrow\colon X\to PX
denote the upperset arrow from

Example

4.5
.
The arrow
P(\uparrow)\colon P^{2}X\to PX
associated to
\uparrow
under the
functor
P\colon\mathscr{E}^{op}\to\mathscr{E}
is the unique one making the
diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times PX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times P^{2}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{PX\times P^{2}X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times P(\uparrow)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uparrow\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{PX}}

commute. By this universal property, the construction of each
\in
-morphism, and naturality, it follows that each component

P(\uparrow)_{C}(\theta)\colon\mathbf{y}C\times X\Rightarrow\Omega
has the
action

P(\uparrow)_{C}(\theta)_{D}(f,a)=\theta_{D}(f,a)

where
\theta\colon\mathbf{y}C\times PX\Rightarrow\Omega
and
f\colon D\to C
is an arrow and
a\in XD
is any element. ∎

Example 4.7

(Necessity Operator)
.

Let
(X,r)
again denote a relation in a presheaf category
\mathscr{E}=[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}]
. The interior operator
\mathsf{int}\colon PX\to PX
can
be described in the following way on the basis of our computations of

P(\uparrow)
and
\downarrow
above. That is, each component

\mathsf{int}_{C}\colon PX(C)\to PX(C)
acts on transformation
\theta\colon\mathbf{y}C\times X\Rightarrow\Omega
, yielding a new transformation

\mathsf{int}_{C}(\theta)
of the same form. By the descriptions in

Example

4.5

and
Construction

4.3
immediately above
and the definition
\mathsf{int}=P(\uparrow)\circ\downarrow
, this is the
composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{y}C\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{y}C\times PC}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\uparrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\downarrow_{C}\!(\theta)}

whose components

\mathsf{int}_{C}(\theta)_{D}\colon\mathscr{C}(D,C)\times XD\to\Omega(D)

have action

\downarrow\!\!_{C}(\theta)_{D}(f,\uparrow\!\!_{D}(a))=(\uparrow\!\!_{D}(a)\leq\theta\cdot f)

specifically by
Equation

4.9
where

f\colon D\to C
is any arrow and
a\in XD
is any element. So, more
colloquially, the value of the
D
-component of
\mathsf{int}_{C}(\theta)
on
such a pair
(f,a)
is the sieve of arrows
g\colon B\to D
such that

\uparrow_{D}\!\!(a)\cdot g\leq\theta\cdot fg
holds in
\Omega(B)
. This
is evidently a statement that the sieve on the left side of the inequality
is contained in the sieve on the right. In the special case that we have

\phi\colon X\to\Omega
and we consider its transpose

\hat{\phi}\colon 1\to PX
, each such component therefore has the action

\downarrow\!\!_{C}(\hat{\phi}(\ast))_{D}(f,\uparrow\!\!_{D}(a))=(\uparrow\!\!_{D}(a)\leq\hat{\phi}_{C}(\ast)\cdot f).

In particular, both sides of the inequality on the right are transformations

\mathbf{y}D\times X\Rightarrow\Omega
and
\leq
is the order on
PX
. The
value of the inequality is the sieve of arrows
g\colon B\to D
such that

\uparrow\!\!_{D}(a)\cdot g\leq\hat{\phi}_{C}(\ast)\cdot fg
holds in
\Omega(B)
.
Each side of the last inequality are transformations

\mathbf{y}B\times X\to\Omega
, which on components
A
for any

h\colon A\to B
and
b\in XA
have the actions

(\uparrow\!\!_{D}(a)\cdot g)_{A}(h,b)=(a\cdot gh\leq_{r}b)

in the order on
X
by
Equation

4.8
; and also

(\hat{\phi}_{C}(\ast)\cdot fg)_{A}(h,b)=\phi_{A}(b)

by
Equation

4.6
. So, in summary, what we
have derived is the statement that the sieve
(a\cdot gh\leq_{r}b)
as a
set must be contained in the sieve
\phi_{A}(b)
for all appropriate elements

a,b
and arrows
g,h
. We can now derive the forcing semantics of
\Box

in the presheaf topos directly. By transposing and just looking at the
diagonal component where
C=D
, we end up with the membership statement

(1_{X}\in\uparrow\!\!_{C}(a))\leq(1_{X}\in\hat{\phi}_{C}(\ast)\cdot f).

The
\leq
is equivalently the implication operator on
\Omega
, so if we
ask that this is true of an element
b\in XC
, we have that the implication

b\in\uparrow\!\!_{C}(a)\text{ implies }b\in\hat{\phi}_{C}(\ast)\cdot f

holds by usual forcing semantics. But by transposing, the antecedent is

a\leq b
in the order on
XC
, while the consequent is
\phi_{C}(b)
. This
yields the necessity forcing statement in

Proposition

4.1
. ∎

4.2
The Indistinguishability Interpretation

This subsection will give an epistemic interpretation to the forcing statements
of the previous development. Partly this is motivated by
epistemic modal
logic
and partly this is suggested by the histories presheaf itself. For on
the one hand epistemic modal logic is modeled by Kripke frames and its
axiomatics are the systems (S4.2) and (S5). On the other hand, the histories
presheaf, involving histories and events, lends itself, by an imaginative leap,
to the view that propositions might be true or
verifiable
from the
perspective of a particular event and in a certain history. In fact, our
semantics are precisely that a space-time event witnesses a necessary
proposition in a particular history if, and only if, it witnesses the
proposition itself in any other history obviously undivided at that event. So,
in a manner of speaking, a proposition is necessarily true in a particular
history at an event if, and only if, the proposition appears to be the case
from the perspective of the event in any history indistinguishable from the the
one in question.

Epistemic logic
[
Hin62
]
,
[
vDHvdHK15
]
is the modal logic of
knowledge and belief. There are usually two operators
K_{a}\phi
and
B_{a}\phi

glossed as
agent
a
knows that

\phi
and
agent
a
believes
that

\phi
. At least
K
tends to be asked to satisfy the comonad axioms. For
this reason, there is an analogy between knowledge and modal necessity. In fact

[
BBOS19
, §2.3]
makes this connection precise: topological
interior models knowledge and open sets model evidence. Belief is then modeled
by the closure of the interior
[
BBOS19
, §3.2]
. When
K
is asked
to have a dual operator, usually classical (S5) provides the associated
axiomatics. Equivalence relations then comprise the natural class of Kripke
models. The
indistinguishability interpretation
of modal epistemic
statements originated with
[
Leh84
]
. In this set-up, there is a
family of equivalence relations
\sim_{i}
parameterized by epistemic agents
i

of the model. Quoting
[
Leh84
, §]
directly:

“The intuitive meaning of
\sim_{i}
is the following: two histories
\sigma
and
\tau
are equivalent with respect to
i
and
k
if the knowledge that person
i
has gathered about the world and its history up to instant
k
cannot enable him to distinguish between
\sigma
and
\tau
. In other words if the real history is
\sigma
, then, for all that person
i
knows at instant
k
, the real history could as well be
\tau
.”

So, a modal statement
K_{i}\phi
is true for a history
\sigma
if, and only if,

\phi
is true in every history
\tau
related to
\sigma
under the provided
equivalence relation for that agent. In other words, as in the quote, an agent
is said to know that
\phi
in a particular history if
\phi
appears to be the
case in every history indistinguishable from it.

In the following, we thus allow ourselves to think of the parameterizing
category
\mathscr{C}
as a system of agents and their relations. We think of the
values
XC
associated to agents
C\in\mathscr{C}
as sets of histories to which
they have access or belong. Specializing to the case of our world
\mathscr{W}
,
this is exactly what the histories presheaf does. And it is not too much of a
stretch to view point events as agents since anyway observers have definite
space-time coordinates in a given inertial frame. There is further warrant for
it in the intuitive interpretation of the intuitionistic semantics of

[
Kri63
, §1.1]
, which we quote as follows:

“We intend the nodes
\mathbf{H}
to represent points in time (or “evidental situation”), at which we may have various pieces of information. If, at a particular point
\mathbf{H}
in time, we have enough information to prove a proposition
A
, we say that
\phi(A,\mathbf{H})=\mathbf{T}
; if we lack such information, we say that
\phi(A,\mathbf{H})=\mathbf{F}
. If
\phi(A,\mathbf{H})=\mathbf{T}
we can say that
A
has been
verified
as the point
\mathbf{H}
in time; if
\phi(A,\mathbf{H})=\mathbf{F}
, then
A
has
not been verified
at
\mathbf{H}
.”

In other words, the nodes, i.e. objects, of the underlying system, i.e.
category, are moments in (space-) time which represent or have attached to
them, or in which are available, a situation or configuration of information
that serves as evidence for the potential confirmation of a proposition under
consideration. It should be noted that in the quote
\phi
stands for the
Kripke model itself associating
\mathbf{T}
or
\mathbf{F}
to the proposition

A
and the stage
\mathbf{H}
. So, that its value is
\mathbf{F}
means only
that
A
has not been verified at
\mathbf{H}
(given the information available
at that instant), but leaves open the possibility of being verified at some
later stage. For this reason, an
\mathbf{F}
does not count as disconfirmation
of a proposition at
\mathbf{H}
.

In any case, we shall proceed as though point events
e
in branching
space-time
\mathscr{W}
represent, or correspond to,
evidential situations
,
potentially available to any agent or observer occupying that space-time
position, by means of which propositions
\phi\colon X\to\Omega
may be
verified. Histories of course are the elements of the values of the histories
presheaf, so we must take account of alternatives and branching as well: for a
given proposition
\phi\colon H\to\Omega
, we take
\phi_{e}(h)
to represent
the value of whether
\phi
is or has been
verified at
e
in
h
.
Perhaps better, since the logic of topos is not Boolean, is that it is the

extent to which
such a verification has been made. This viewpoint
comports well with the indistinguishability interpretation. As above, we denote
by
\Box\phi
the morphism

\Box\phi:=\mathsf{int}(\hat{\phi})\colon 1\to PH

where
\hat{\phi}\colon 1\to PH
is the name of
\phi
given by exponential
transpose. By interior semantics in
Proposition

3.8
and
Proposition

4.1
we
then have

\displaystyle e\Vdash\Box\phi(h)

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }e\Vdash h\in_{H}\mathsf{int}(\hat{\phi})

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }e\Vdash h\approx_{e}h^{\prime}\text{ implies }e\Vdash h^{\prime}\in_{H}\hat{\phi}

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }e\Vdash h\approx_{e}h^{\prime}\text{ implies }e\Vdash\phi(h^{\prime}).

Thus, our gloss on
e\Vdash\Box\phi(h)
is the statement that a point event

e
forces
necessarily

\phi
at a history
h
containing
e
if, and
only if,
e
forces
\phi
at every history
h^{\prime}
obviously undivided from
h

at
e
. More colloquially, then,
\phi
is necessarily true of
h
at an event

e
if, and only if, it is true of all histories obviously undivided from
h

at
e
. Interpreting
obviously undivided
as the relationship encoding
the fact that
e
does not, or cannot, distinguish between any two histories
for which
e
is not a choice point, the statement
e\Vdash\Box\phi(h)
thus
says that the proposition
\phi
is necessarily true of
h
at the event
e

if, and only if, it is true of any history indistinguishable from
h
at
e
.
Likewise for the possibility operator, we shall denote by
\Diamond\phi
the
morphism

\Diamond\phi:=\mathsf{cl}(\hat{\phi})\colon 1\to\ PH.

By the closure semantics of
Corollary

3.11
, we have
then that

\displaystyle e\Vdash\Diamond\phi(h)

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }e\Vdash h\in_{H}\mathsf{cl}(\hat{\phi})

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }e\Vdash h\approx_{e}h^{\prime}\text{ and }e\Vdash h^{\prime}\in_{H}\hat{\phi}\text{ for some }h^{\prime}

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }e\Vdash h\approx_{e}h^{\prime}\text{ and }e\Vdash\phi(h^{\prime})\text{ for some }h^{\prime}.

That is,
e
forces
possibly

\phi
at
h
if, and only if, there is
some history
h^{\prime}
indistinguishable from
h
at
e
of which
\phi
is true at
stage
e
. So, to distinguish from necessity, possibility is just the statement
that the proposition is true of some history, potentially distinct from the one
under consideration, but which is indistinguishable from it at the current
stage, of which the proposition is true at the current stage.

4.3
First-Order Considerations

In this final subsection, we begin the project of examining first-order
formulas in light of the semantics developed so far. Our first question is
whether our models validate the
Barcan formulas
of
[
Bar47
]
.
Two of these are discussed below in
Corollary

4.9
and the
subsequent examples. We shall look finally at the existence predicates of

[
Sco79
]
in light of these results and the actualist thesis

Equation

A
. Throughout we shall use the recipe for
formation of quantified formula in toposes as discussed in

Example

2.8
. Our main tool will be the
presheaf semantics in

Proposition

4.1
.

The first result shows that in any topos, the interior operator on the
subobject classifier is trivial.

Proposition 4.8
.

In any topos,
\mathsf{int}\colon\Omega\to\Omega
is the identity.

Proof.

The downset arrow
1\to P1
is the generic subobject

\top\colon 1\to P1=\Omega
. In the following diagram, the top square
commutes by the definition of
P(\uparrow)
as a transpose, while the
triangle commutes by the definition of
\downarrow
also as a transpose:

On the whole, this realizes
P(\uparrow)\circ\downarrow
as the transpose
of
\top\Rightarrow_{\Omega}1
. But it is easy to see that this arrow must
be the identity on
\Omega
, for the square in the diagram

\hbox to162.17pt{\vbox to54.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 81.08316pt\lower-28.35971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

are both pullbacks. The rightmost is a pullback by the definition of the
implication operator; the leftmost is one by a direct computation. Since

(\top\Rightarrow 1)=1
, this shows the result by uniqueness.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

In any topos, with
\Box
interpreted as interior, any preordered object
(X,r)
validates two of the so-called
Barcan Formulas
, namely,

1.

\forall x\Box\phi(x)\supset\Box\forall x\phi(x)

2.

\exists x\Box\phi(x)\supset\Box\exists x\phi(x)
.

Proof.

We shall prove the first one; that of the second is analogous.

Proposition

4.8
shows that for any truth value

\omega\colon 1\to\Omega
, we have
\Box\omega=\omega
. In particular,
this is the case if
\omega
is any closed formula. Thus,

\Box\forall_{X}\phi=\forall_{X}\phi
holds for any
\phi\colon X\to\Omega

in the topos. It therefore suffices to prove that

\forall_{X}\Box\phi\Rightarrow_{\Omega}\forall_{X}\phi
holds, or equivalently
that
\forall_{X}\Box\phi\leq\forall_{X}\phi
holds in the order on
\Omega
,
but this follows immediately. For
\Box\phi\leq\phi
always holds by

Lemma

3.14
and
\forall_{X}
, being a right adjoint, is in
particular a functor, that is, order-preserving.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

With regard to existential quantification, in fancier language, this
corollary implies that the
de re
modality
\exists x\Box\phi

implies the
de dicto
modality
\Box\exists x\phi
in the sense that
any preordered object
(X,r)
in a topos validates the implication

\exists x\Box\phi\supset\Box\exists\phi
. This is expected; as
remarked in
[
AK08
, §4.1]
this formula is provable in
first-order S4, hence it is valid in all topological semantics including
that arising from the Alexandrov topology. The point is that this
Alexandrov topology result caries over into any topos. What is suprising is
that the first formula
\forall x\Box\phi(x)\supset\Box\forall x\phi(x)

is validated in any such model. For in
[
AK08
, §4.1]
,
there is a sheaf-theoretic counter model. Thus, our result shows that this
Barcan formula is true for a restricted class of topological models and
holds in any such model in any topos. Concerning the converses, that of the
first formula is provable in first-order S4, whereas that of the second has
a counter-model.∎

Example 4.11
.

We shall invalidate
\Box\exists x\phi\supset\exists x\Box\phi
in the
category of sets. Let
\mathscr{E}=[\mathbf{1},\mathbf{Set}]=\mathbf{Set}
and consider the
set
\mathbb{N}
of natural numbers viewed as a poset with its usual
ordering. Let
\phi\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
denote the proposition

is even
. In
\mathbf{Set}
, the operators
\Box
and
\Diamond
have their
usual description as Alexandrov interior and closure from

Equation

2.2
and

Equation

2.3
. In this case,

\Box\phi
is the set

\Box\phi=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid\text{for all }m\in\mathbb{N}\text{ if }n\leq m\text{ then }m\in\phi\}

which is of course equal to
\emptyset
since for any given natural number,
there is an odd one greater than or equal to it. Additionally, we can use
the computation of ‘
\exists
’ from
[
MM92
, §I.9]
to
describe
\exists_{\mathbb{N}}\colon P\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
. In the case of
our
\phi\subset\mathbb{N}
, we have

\exists_{\mathbb{N}}\phi=\{y\in\mathbf{1}\mid\text{there is }n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ such that }n\in\phi\}=1

since there is an even natural number and

\exists_{\mathbb{N}}\Box\phi=\{y\in\mathbf{1}\mid\text{there is }n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ such that }n\in\Box\phi\}=0

since again for every natural number there is an odd natural number greater
than or equal to it. So, the
de re
statement will imply the
de
dicto
since
0\leq 1
, but the converse, namely,

\exists_{\mathbb{N}}\phi\leq\exists_{\mathbb{N}}\Box\phi
is impossible. ∎

Likewise, the possibility operator on
\Omega=P1
is trivial. Recall that
the closure operator appears in
Definition

3.4
as

\mathsf{cl}=\bigvee\exists_{\downarrow}
. Thus, the proof of the next result
relies partly upon
Construction

3.1
which developed the
internal join operator.

Proposition 4.12
.

In any topos
\mathsf{cl}\colon\Omega\to\Omega
is the identity morphism.

Proof.

First note that
\exists_{\downarrow}
is actually the transpose of the meet

\wedge\colon\Omega\times\Omega\to\Omega
. This is because we are looking
at the order on the terminal,
1
, which is the isomorphism

1\cong 1\times 1
. As a result,
\downarrow\colon 1\to P1
as in the
diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\times P1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\times 1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\downarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

must be
\top\colon 1\to\Omega
. So,
\exists_{\downarrow}=\exists_{\top}

is formed as in
Section

2.2
. That is, first
take the classifying morphism as on the bottom of the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\times P1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P1\times P1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle 1,\top\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\top,1\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{1}}

Note that
\in_{1}
is an isomorphism, meaning that
\langle 1,\top\rangle

is the classified subobject; likewise
\wedge
is
defined
as the
classifying morphism of
\langle\top,\top\rangle
. Now,

\exists_{\downarrow}=\exists_{\top}
is therefore the transpose of
\wedge
,
as claimed. Now, put this together with the construction of

\bigvee\colon P\Omega\to\Omega
. We have a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\times P1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\times P\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\times P\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\times P1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega\times P1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\bigvee}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\in_{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\exists_{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\exists_{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wedge}

Note that the top-left square commutes by the construction of
\bigvee
as

\Omega^{\top}
. But the point of presenting this diagram is that

\top\wedge 1=1
in any topos. This is well-known in propositional logic,
but for the record, the proof is easy, just noting that the following is a
pullback:

\hbox to165.42pt{\vbox to49.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 84.3388pt\lower-26.58192pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-81.08316pt}{-18.88196pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -112.19 -26.13)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

In any case, by uniqueness of transposes, this proves that

\bigvee\exists_{\top}=1
must hold.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

Any preordered object
(X,r)
in a topos
\mathscr{E}
validates the formulas

1.

\Diamond\forall x\phi\supset\forall x\Diamond\phi

2.

\Diamond\exists x\phi\supset\exists x\Diamond\phi

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of
Corollary

4.9
again
using the previous proposition, the functoriality of quantifiers, and the
fact that the order validates
\phi\supset\Diamond\phi
.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

The converses of the formulas in
Corollary

4.13
are also of
interest. These are
\forall x\Diamond\phi\supset\Diamond\forall x\phi

and
\exists x\Diamond\phi\supset\Diamond\exists x\phi
. In general, the
former fails in our models in toposes whereas the latter is provable in
first-order S4
[
AK08
, §4.1]
. In our system, the latter
takes the form
\exists_{X}\Diamond\phi\leq\exists_{X}\phi
in the order on

\Omega
since
\Diamond=1
on
\Omega
by

Proposition

4.12
. Its validity might be
surprising, since it glosses as
if there is something for which a
proposition is possibly the case then there is something for which it is
the case
, which we are tempted to sloganize as
possibilities are
actual
, or perhaps
possibilities are real
. The following example
invalidates the universally quantified formula and sheds some light on the
truth of the existential one. ∎

Example 4.15
.

Return to the situation of
Example

2.19
,
namely, the simple branching space-time
\mathscr{W}
with four point events
and three nontrivial arrows. Again, the histories presheaf
H
makes the
assignments summarized in the diagram

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Let
\phi\colon H\to\Omega
denote the classifying morphism of
H_{1}
, the
subobject on the left. Think of this as the proposition
h_{1}

occurs
. Our claim is that the formula
\forall x\Diamond\phi\supset\forall x\phi
is not valid in this model. This formula is interpreted as
an implication, or equivalently, as an inequality in the order on
\Omega
;
thus, the implication is validated if there is a valid containment between
(the subobjects associated to) the antecedent
\forall_{H}\Diamond\phi
and
the consequent
\forall_{H}\phi
. It takes only one component where this
fails toevidence that the implication cannot hold. So, consider the values
at the event
e_{-1}
. By the computation of the effect of internal
universal quantification in presheaf categories (cf.

[
AKK14
, Example 1.3]
and
[
MM92
, Equations III.8.(14)-III.8.(15)]
), we have

(\forall_{H}\phi)_{e_{-1}}=\bigvee\{s\in\Omega(e_{-1})\mid u^{*}s\leq\phi_{e_{-1}}(u,b)\text{ for all }u\colon y\to e_{-1},b\in H_{y}\}

and

(\forall_{H}\Diamond\phi)_{e_{-1}}=\bigvee\{s\in\Omega(e_{-1})\mid u^{*}s\leq\Diamond\phi_{e_{-1}}(u,b)\text{ for all }u\colon y\to e_{-1},b\in H_{y}\}.

There are two sieves on
e_{-1}
, namely,
\displaystyle\Omega(e_{-1})=\{\top_{e_{-1}},\emptyset\}
. Moreover, there is only one arrow

y\to e_{-1}
, namely, the identity. So, we have that
u^{*}s=s
is either

\top
or
\emptyset
and consequently

(\forall_{H}\phi)_{e_{-1}}=\bigvee\{s\in\Omega(e_{-1})\mid s\leq\phi_{e_{-1}}(h)\text{ for all }h\in H_{e_{-1}}\}

and

(\forall_{H}\Diamond\phi)_{e_{-1}}=\bigvee\{s\in\Omega(e_{-1})\mid s\leq\Diamond\phi_{e_{-1}}(h)\text{ for all }h\in H_{e_{-1}}\}.

But on the one hand,
\phi_{e_{-1}}(h_{2})=\emptyset
holds by construction
of
\phi
, meaning that
\top
is not in the former union. On the other
hand,
\Diamond\phi_{e_{-1}}(h_{1})=\Diamond\phi_{e_{-1}}(h_{2})=\top

both hold by the presheaf forcing semantics of
\Diamond
in
Proposition

4.1
since

h_{1}\approx_{e_{-1}}h_{2}
holds. Therefore,
\top
is in the latter union
and so we conclude that

(\forall_{H}\phi)_{e_{-1}}=\emptyset\qquad(\forall_{H}\Diamond\phi)_{e_{-1}}=\top

Therefore, the latter is not contained in the former, and we have our
counter-model of
\forall x\Diamond\phi\supset\forall x\phi
as was
claimed. Note that this is the simplest component of the involved presheaf
that invalidates the formula. But the components at
e_{2}
do the job too.
For, using the computations of the effect of internal ‘
\forall
’ above,
one can see that

(\forall_{H}\phi)_{e_{2}}=\emptyset\qquad\text{whereas}\qquad(\forall_{H}\Diamond\phi)_{e_{2}}=\{\beta\gamma\}

since again
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are obviously undivided at
e_{-1}
. ∎

A final application now concerns the
existence predicate
of, for
example,
[
Sco79
]
. This is indicated by the symbol ‘
\mathsf{E}
’
applied to terms
t
, and resulting in a predicate
\mathsf{E}(t)
having the
intuitive meaning of
t
exists
. This predicate is used in the reference
in the formulation of theories requiring partial operations such as the
theories of categories and of local rings. For simplicity
1
1

1

See

[
Sco79
, Metatheorem 2.3]
for details on the relationship between
equality, equivalence and existence in this system.
, we shall take the
existence prediate to be defined as

\mathsf{E}(t)\leftrightsquigarrow\exists y(t=y)

for a term of type
X
, a variable of type
X
. This is also an

actualist definition
of the existence predicate

[
Men22
, §2.2.2]
. Note that
\mathsf{E}(t)
is thus
\Omega
-valued
but not necessarily a truth value. Hence it is not the case that
\Box\mathsf{E}(t)=\mathsf{E}(t)
.

In the indistinguishability interpretation, we have the following.
In any presheaf topos
\mathscr{E}=[\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathbf{Set}]
, the forcing semantics of
existential quantification for an preordered object
(X,r)
yields

C\Vdash\exists x(a=_{X}x)\text{ if, and only if, }C\Vdash a=_{X}b\text{ for some }b\in XC.

But the right side is just ordinary equality in
XC
. Hence we have

C\Vdash\exists x(a=_{X}x)\text{ if, and only if, }a=b\text{ for some }b\in XC

which is always true, since the forcing statement assumes that we are provided
with the
a\in XC
. On the other hand, the formula
\exists x\Box(y=_{X}x)
has
the forcing semantics

\displaystyle C\Vdash\exists x\Box(a=_{X}x)

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }C\Vdash\exists x\Box(a=_{X}x)

(4.10)

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }C\Vdash\Box(a=_{X}b)\text{ for some }b\in XC

(4.11)

\displaystyle\text{ if, and only if, }C\Vdash(b\approx b^{\prime})\text{ implies }C\Vdash(a=_{X}b^{\prime})\text{ for all }b^{\prime}\in XC

(4.12)

by
Proposition

4.1
. The gloss on
this is thus that there exists something necessarily equal to
a
if, and only
if, there is something
b
such that
a
is equal to everything in the
equivalence class of
b
under the relation on
X
. In the epistemic
interpretation, there is something necessarily equal to
a
if, and only if,
there is something
b
such that
a
is equal to everything indistinguishable
from
b
. But again
=_{X}
reduces to ordinary equality when applied in each set

XC
since
=_{X}
classifies the diagonal. Hence we conclude that

C\Vdash\exists x\Box(a=_{X}x)\text{ if, and only if, }C\Vdash(b\approx b^{\prime})\text{ implies }C\Vdash(a=b^{\prime})\text{ for all }b^{\prime}\in XC

which we think of as a very strong kind of indistinguishability: there exists
something
b
necessarily equal to
a
if and only if
a
is equal to
everything indistinguishable from
b
. In general this will not be true in
presheaf toposes as long as
\approx
is a non-trivial relation, such as a
genuinely objectively indeterminate branching space-time where the
quantification is over histories
h
. In general this should not be surprising,
for given the relation, there is no saying that
a
is even equal to everything
indistinguishable from itself. Generally, that is, identity implies
indistinguishability but not conversely, unless we are in a special system
where equivalence implies identity. Perhaps this is of interest in more general
toposes such as genuine sheaf categories, which we leave to later developments.

Finally, we observe that the previous developments invalidate actualist thesis

Equation

A
, namely, that
existent potentialities
are actual
. More colloquially, this says that what exists possibly or
potentially exists actually. We formalize this as the formula

\forall x(\Diamond\mathsf{E}(x)\supset\mathsf{E}(x))

which is of couse equal to

\forall x\Diamond\mathsf{E}(x)\supset\forall x\mathsf{E}(x)

since in any topos
\forall_{X}
is an internal functor. But this has a
counter-model in the form on non-trivial branching above.

Example 4.16
.

The result of
Example

4.15
we can apply to the special case of
\phi:=\mathsf{E}(x)
which shows that in the simple example of non-trivial branching the antecedent of
\forall x\Diamond\mathsf{E}(x)\supset\forall x\mathsf{E}(x)
does not imply the consequent. We interpret this as a denial of the actualist thesis that possible existents are actual. Note that this does not necessarily validate possibilism. ∎

5
Prospectus

The results and perspectives in this paper offer a starting point for some
further avenues of inquiry and raise some questions which we address here
briefly in closing.

1.

The underlying poset
our world
has been thought of as imposing
a causal rather than temporal relation on point events. However, there is
no specific reason to restrict the general formalism to only this case.
That is, supposing that the underlying order is not necessarily
anti-symmetric, we have a reasonable model of temporally-related events.
Histories then are temporal histories rather than causal histories.

Obviously undivided
is still some kind of relation. Supposing
reasonable conditions under which it is symmetric, we have again an
internal equivalence relation which may again be given an epistemic
interpretation. Thus, on the one hand, we have a temporal parameterization
along with an object modeling an epistemic
indistinguishability

relation. The extent to which this promises a unified framework for a
treatment of epistemic and temporal modal logic
[
DNR15
]
will be
left to later investigations.

2.

The relation
obviously undivided
defined on the histories
presheaf could be viewed as an object of the bicategory, or better the
double category, of relations in the topos of presheaves on
our
world
. A more
relational view
would be consistent with the
perspective of
[
Kis17
]
which views modalities as certain kinds
of relations and the adjunctions involving modalities and quantifiers are
studied there in some detail. Many of these features are formally
replicable in the setting of a
double category of relations

[
Lam22
]
and thus the question arises as to the extent to which
the essential constructions of this work are recovered in a more abstract
double-categorical framework. Any progress on this question would need to
be compared to, and probably motivated by, the direct bicategorical
treatment of modality appearing in
[
Her11
]
. Motivating examples
in that study are bicategories of relations and of partial maps, both of
which are known to be abstract
bicategories of relations
in the
sense of
[
CW87
]
which are recovered as examples of
locally posetal cartesian equipments in
[
Lam22
]
.

3.

Such a general framework could afford applications to, and embrace
different approaches to,
formal epistemology
. For example, the
geometric semantics of knowledge and belief in
[
BBOS19
]

plausibly fall under this picture. Common knowledge
[
Leh84
]
is
potentially modeled using ordinary morphisms of the underlying category of
a double category which model subcollections of agents. This can
potentially be generalized to give relationships between modal models where
models are proarrows and ordinary arrows induce transition morphims from
the restriction and extension constructions of the ambient equipment.
Likewise, synthetic probability theory
[
Fri19
]
has not yet been
phrased in double-categorical terms, but the
Markov categories
of
that subject bear striking resemblences to cartesian bicategories.
Commutative comonoids are an example. In general double categories allow
for the unified description of deterministic and non-deterministic
processes. They are thus a natural forum to try to recast the results and
methods of that subject. A satisfactory double-categorical synthetic
probability theory could almost certainly be based on some version of the
axioms for a cartesian equipment. Thus, we envision a further application of such a framekwork for formal epistemology to a description of probabilistic epistemic logic
[
DS15
]
.

4.

The philosophical import of the results of the paper and especially
those in the last section could certainly be studied further. A more
detailed analysis of the possibilism/actualism question seems in order.
Additionally, questions related to
necessary entities
and

essential properties

[
Par76
]
, may profitably be modeled
using the formal methods introduced in this paper.
